\section{Introduction} 

\david{I re-wrote the opening paragraph a bit. We referred to estimation when we don't obtain any estimation results, etc.} \paul{I feel like the first few lines flow less naturally now. I don't see the link between the first sentence and the next one.} \paul{"Estimation" does not seem wrong to me. Aren't we estimating the support of $\beta^*$. Maybe "inference" instead of "estimation"?}

High-dimensional techniques designed for settings where there are many parameters relative to the sample size $n$ frequently rely on strict assumptions on sparsity and signal strength.\\
\\
%In many modern applications, the sample size is insufficient to reliably estimate the parameters in a model. This limitation has motivated the development of high-dimensional techniques that frequently rely on strict assumptions on sparsity and signal strength. %Yet, often, external information is available and can be leveraged to enhance parameter estimation. 
\paul{My proposal as opening paragraph, basically the previous one but with inference instead of estimation:}
In many modern applications, the sample size is insufficient to reliably do inference. This limitation has motivated the development of high-dimensional techniques that frequently rely on strict assumptions on sparsity and signal strength. Yet, often, external information is available and can be leveraged to enhance inference.
%Our motivation stems from the observation that, often, 
In particular, external information can guide our decisions regarding which parameters should be included in a given model. In regression, this information may originate from the inherent nature of each variable (e.g., clinical history versus genomic markers, sociodemographics versus job history) or external data that distinguishes relevant variables from irrelevant ones (e.g., functional annotations on genes). 
Our paper investigates this concept in the the Gaussian Sequence Model (GSM) and linear regression, where external information helps partition variables into groups with potentially distinct characteristics.

Incorporating external information is often advocated within the framework of data integration, as joint learning can lead to more accurate inference compared to analyzing datasets separately. Indeed, numerous applied works have employed external information to guide inference. For instance, \cite{stingo:2011}, \cite{quintana}, and \cite{cassese:2014} proposed Bayesian variable selection methods for gene expression, where prior probabilities for non-zero coefficients depend on biological knowledge and meta-covariates. Additionally, \cite{chen_tinghuei:2021} predicted disease outcomes by allowing LASSO penalties to depend on functional annotation categories. Beyond regression, node and edge covariates have been incorporated in \cite{peterson} and \cite{jewson2023graphical} to drive edge inclusion in Gaussian graphical models, while \cite{schiavon2022generalized} used meta-covariates to determine non-zero loadings in factor models. In causal analysis, covariates of interest (referred to as treatments) guide the inclusion of other covariates based on their degree of association with the treatments \cite{belloni2014inference,antonelli2021bayesian}. Collectively, empirical evidence consistently demonstrates improved structural learning when external data is suitably integrated. However, despite this empirical success, a theoretical framework explaining precisely why this occurs remains elusive.

Here we show that incorporating external information allows pushing the theoretical limits under which consistent variable selection is possible, understood as recovering the true model with probability going to 1 as $n$ and the number of variables $p$ grow. Previous literature has extensively explored how high-dimensional variable selection is constrained by inherent characteristics of the dataset, such as $n$, $p$, the signal strength, the correlation between variables and the number of variables truly associated to the outcome \cite{buhlmannvandegeerbook,overviewvarsel,handbookbayesvarsel,wainwrighthighdimbook}. In this paper we analyse how conditions for consistency can be relaxed in the presence of external information. We focus on variable selection but using external information is also expected to benefit parameter estimation and prediction problems. 

To make our ideas concrete, consider the variable selection problem in the linear model with Gaussian noise:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linearmodel}
	\bs y \;=\; \bs X \bs \beta^{\star} + \bs\epsilon,\qquad \bs \epsilon \sim N(0,\sigma^2 I_n),
\end{equation}
where $\bs X\in \R^{n\times p}$, $\bs y\in \R^n$, and $\sigma >0$. A large class of methods operate by penalizing the size of an estimated $\hat{\bs  \beta}$. For instance, penalized likelihood methods are based on optimizing the log-likelihood plus a penalty term driven by the $\ell_q$ "norm" of $\hat{\bs  \beta}$ for $q\in [0,1]$ or folded concave penalties \citep{lasso,overviewvarsel,bertsimas}. 
In Bayesian settings, variable selection is often based on posterior model probabilities that are directly connected to $\ell_0$ penalties \citep{BIC,EBIC,rossell2022concentration}. 
%In frequentist settings, recent advances in discrete optimization have also rendered $\ell_0$ penalization much more computationally efficient \cite{bertsimas,mazumdergroup}.
% Roughly speaking, the penalty must grow with the number of truly inactive variables ($\beta_j^\star=0$) to avoid selecting an overfitting model, but it must also be small enough to avoid missing small signals \cite{buhlmannvandegeerbook,handbookbayesvarsel,wainwrighthighdimbook}.
% Those requirements results in so-called betamin conditions that involve the smallest non-zero absolute parameter value $\beta_{\min}^{\star}= \min_j |\beta_j^{\star}|$.  

We study the case where the external information partitions the $p$ variables into $b$ blocks denoted $B_j$, $j=1,\ldots,b$ across which key characteristics such as the level of sparsity or signal strength may vary. We introduce block $\ell_0$ penalties that allow modulating the strength of the penalty in each block. Unlike standard $\ell_0$ penalties, like BIC \cite{BIC}, RIC \cite{RIC} or EBIC \cite{EBIC}, block penalties are non-exchangeable in the sense that the complexity penalty for adding a variable $j$ may depend on its block $B_j$.

\paul{Reorganized the intro to have this contribution paragraph.}
Our main contributions are as follows. We show that block-based non-exchangeable $\ell_0$ penalties soften
%the use of non-exchangeable $\ell_0$ penalties driven by external information softens
the theoretical conditions for consistent model recovery in the Gaussian sequence model and in linear regression. %high-dimensional linear models. 
Specifically, %We further demonstrate 
that it is possible for an oracle to set block-based penalties so that variable selection consistency is either attained where otherwise not possible or at a faster rate. Another important contribution is that we introduce data-based variable selection procedures, %with informed block penalties 
based on information criteria and empirical Bayes, that realize the theoretical benefits without requiring an oracle. Other more minor contributions include a tight necessary condition on signal strength in high-dimensional orthogonal settings and consistency results for the EBIC in a wider class of asymptotic regimes than proved in \cite{EBIC} and \cite{ebicdiverging}.

Our work has connections with ideas from multiple hypothesis testing. In this line of research, \cite{genovese2006} proposed a false discovery rate (FDR) %adjustment 
procedure in which p-values are weighted based on prior information. They show that if the weights are positively associated to the null hypotheses being false, their procedure improves power. Subsequent works discussed oracle choices of weights either based on external information or derived from the data %with weighting based on external information and weights derived from the data 
\cite{roederwasserman,basu}. 
Adaptive FDR methods \cite{blanchard} estimate the %Another stream of FDR control methods are called adaptive \cite{blanchard} and use estimate of the
proportion of truly null hypotheses to improve statistical power.
Recently, \cite{ramdas2019prior} combined external information %weighting 
and adaptivity in a group-based FDR control. 
Relative to this work, we study %focus on studying 
variable selection consistency in high-dimensional regression %a high-dimensional regression setting 
as well as the associated conditions on sparsity and signal strength.

\david{I added a paragraph on Scarlett et al.}
\color{blue} Also related to our work, \cite{scarlett2012compressed} studied compressed sensing when prior information allows splitting parameters into blocks, where one knows the true proportion of non-zero parameters in each block. They show that sparse signal recovery requires smaller $n$ than with exchangeable penalties. Key differences with our work are their assuming independence across covariates, which renders the results inapplicable to regression. Also, they do not consider the sequence model, nor that the proportions of non-zero parameters are unknown in practice.   \color{black} \paul{I would also add that the design is assumed random, and consistency is taken with respect to this source of randomness.}

The paper is organized as follows. Section~\ref{sec:intro_blockpen} introduces block $\ell_0$ penalization. Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} presents its %the theoretical 
variable selection properties %of block penalization 
in the Gaussian sequence model, %, a high-dimensional linear and orthogonal model that generalizes orthogonal linear regression. 
in an oracle setting where the sparsity and betamin conditions are known for all groups.
%We also discuss the benefits of an oracle block penalization in relation to the discriminative power of the external information. 
Those results apply to essentially any penalized likelihood or Bayesian method, including $\ell_0$ and $\ell_1$ penalties, because in that setting selection operates by thresholding \cite{davidomiros,rigollethighdimensional}. Section~\ref{sec:properties_legression} studies block $\ell_0$ penalties in linear regression, and shows analogous benefits to those in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth}.
%analyzes the properties of block $\ell_0$ penalties in the more general linear regression setting where variables are correlated, and shows that the benefits of the penalties are analogous to those discussed in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth}.
%We remark that results in this section
These results can be extended to a wide class of Bayesian variable selection methods. Section~\ref{sec:informed_l0} presents data-based %variable selection 
procedures %with block $\ell_0$ penalties that leverage external information to 
that achieve faster convergence without requiring an oracle. Section~\ref{sec:simulation} shows empirical examples and Section~\ref{sec:discussion} concludes. Proofs are gathered at the end of the paper.
\medskip

\noindent \textbf{Notation:}  
We denote by $\bs \beta\in \R^p$ the parameters of interest and by $\bs \beta^*$ their true values. Let  $V=\{1,\ldots,p\}$. 
%In a linear model parameterized by $\bs \beta\in \R^p$ with true value $\bs \beta^*$, we denote by $V=\{1,\ldots,p\}$ the set of indices of $\bs \beta$. 
For any $A\subseteq V$ and any vector $\bs x\in \R^p$, $\bs x_A$ denotes the subvector of $\bs x$ with entries corresponding to indices in $A$. For any matrix $\bs X \in \R^{n\times p}$, $\bs X_A$ denotes the submatrix of $\bs X$ obtained by selecting the \emph{columns} with indices in $A$. We denote by
$S=\left\{i\in V:\,\,  \beta^{\star}_i \neq 0 \right\}$ the true support of $\bs \beta^{\star}$, its size is $s=|S|$, and hence $p-s$ is the number of truly inactive parameters.
Denote $\beta_{\min}^{\star}=\min_{i \in S}|\beta^{\star}_i|$ the smallest true signal.
We assume that $V$ is partitioned into a fixed number $b$ of disjoint blocks $B_j \subseteq V$ for $j=1,\ldots,b$.
We denote by $S_j=\left\{i\in B_j:\,\,  \beta^{\star}_i \neq 0\right\}$ the set of active parameters in block $B_j$,  $s_j=|S_j|$ its size, $p_j-s_j$ the number of inactive parameters in the block,
and $\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}=\min_{i \in S_j}|\beta^{\star}_i|$.
Given sequences $f(n) > 0$ and $g(n) >0$, $f(n)=O(g(n))$ means that there exists a constant $c<\infty$ such that $f(n) \leq c g(n)$ for all $n \geq n_0$ and some fixed $n_0$.
$f(n)=o(g(n))$ means that $f(n) / g(n) \to 0$, and $f(n)=\Theta(g(n))$ means that $f(n) = O(g(n))$ and $g(n) = O(f(n))$. 
\medskip

\noindent\textbf{Our asymptotic regime:} Although not denoted explicitly, $s_j$, $p_j$ and $\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}$ are  functions of $n$, and so are $s$, $p$ and $\beta_{\min}^{\star}$. We study asymptotic regimes where
%in this paper high-dimensional asymptotic results and assume:
\begin{enumerate}[start=1,label={(A\arabic*)}]
    \item $n \,\to \, \infty$, and the number of blocks $b$ is constant.
    \item For all $j$, $p_j-s_j \,\to \, \infty$.
\end{enumerate}
Assumption A2 implies that $p-s$ goes to infinity but we do not make any assumption on the asymptotic regime linking $n$ and $p$. We do not impose any restriction either on the total numbers of active parameters, $s$, nor across blocks, the $s_j$'s.

\section{Variable selection via informed block penalization}\label{sec:intro_blockpen}

Consider a set of candidates models $\mathcal{M}$ given by subsets $M\subseteq V$ and their corresponding coordinate subspaces
$$
\mathcal{L}_M\;:=\;\{\beta\in \R^p: \beta_i=0\mbox{ if }i\notin M\}.
$$
By definition the true model $S$ is the smallest subset such that $\bs\beta^\star\in L_S$, which for simplicity we assume to be unique \piotr{Two points: (i) S was defined in the notation section as the support of $\beta^*$ and its easiest to just to repeat this definition, (ii) the way it is defined, it is unique so we do not need to assume this.} \paul{I think $S$ is unique as defined too. But David explained in an email on Tue, 1 Oct, 14:27 that S can be non-unique. Based on David's explanation, what can be non-unique is "S" defined as "smallest subset of covariates minimizing ssr" but this is not how we defined it here.}\piotr{Then we need to sort out, which definition we use. Because if $S$ is defined as the support of $\beta^*$ (as here) then this is uniquely defined. Perhaps you means that $\widehat S$ may not be unique?}.
Consider a standard $\ell_0$ selection procedure with penalty $\eta(M)$ that depends on $M$ linearly 
%Consider the class of variable selection procedures based on standard linear $\ell_0$-penalties $\eta$, that is, penalties that depend on the model $M$ linearly 
through its cardinality, $\eta(M)=\kappa |M|$ for some $\kappa>0$. The selected model is
%The selected model is the smallest maximizer, over all $M\in \mathcal{M}$, of the penalized likelihood: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stdl0}
\hat{S}= \arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)\;-\;\kappa |M|\right\},
\end{equation}
where $\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)$ is the log-likelihood function. 
%More generally, consider a fixed partition $B_1/\cdots/B_b$ of $V$.
%, that is, a collection of disjoint subsets $B_j\subseteq V$ whose union is equal to $V$. We fix this partition throughout and $b$ refers to the number of its blocks 
%We consider a procedure that selects a model $M$ for $\bs\beta^\star$ using different penalties for different blocks $B_1,\ldots,B_b$, defining a partition of $V$. 
We study a block-informed procedure that penalizes differently each block $B_1,\ldots,B_b$,
%Fixing $(\kappa_1,\ldots,\kappa_b)$, the selected model $\hat S^b$ is the smallest maximizer, over all $M\in \mathcal{M}$, of the  likelihood penalized with \textbf{block informed} linear $\ell_0$-penalty:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mhat}
  \hat S^b\;\in\;\arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j| \right\},
\end{equation}
where $\kappa_1>0,\ldots,\kappa_b>0$, we denote a model by $M=M_1\cup\cdots\cup M_b$, and $M_j\subseteq B_j$ represents the  selected parameters in block $j$. % that are present in the model $M$.

%The selector $\hat S^b$, which is the main object of our study in this paper,  generalizes variable selection procedures based on standard linear $\ell_0$-penalties: 
Note that $\hat{S}^1$ is the standard $\ell_0$ selector in \eqref{eq:stdl0}.

\section{Gaussian sequence model}\label{sec:properties_orth}
%\section{Properties in the Gaussian sequence model}\label{sec:properties_orth}

%\paul{Added this outline of section and discussion of the assumption $s_j\to \infty$}
We now introduce the Gaussian sequence model, its connection to orthogonal linear regression and discuss that $\hat S^b$ performs block-wise hard thresholding. We also provide its variable selection properties, discuss its benefits in terms of lessening the conditions for consistency variable selection and convergence rates, and illustrate said advantages under different asymptotic regimes.
%In this section, we start by introducing the Gaussian sequence model, its connections to orthogonal linear regression and show that in that setting $\hat S^b$ is a particular instance of a more general class of procedures that perform blockwise hard thresholding. 
%We proceed with a derivation of the variable selection properties of block thresholding in the Gaussian sequence model. We then discuss the benefits they bring in terms of conditions for selection consistency and rates of convergence. %We particularly highlight the impact of the quality of external information and the resulting partition on those benefits. 
%We end with examples exhibiting the benefits under different asymptotic regimes and scenarios of external information.

In this section, in addition to A1-A2, we assume that
%The results and discussion of this section focus on asymptotic regimes in which, in addition to A1 and A2, the following assumption on the numbers of active parameters holds:
\begin{enumerate}[start=3,label={(A\arabic*)}]
    \item $\text{for all } j, \;\; s_j \,\to \, \infty$
\end{enumerate}
Assumption A3 is made to simplify the exposition, and because we derive a new necessary condition on signal strength under A3, but we obtained analogous results when $s_j$'s can be finite (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendixGSMsfinite}).
%Assumption A3 implies that the total number of active parameters $s$ goes to infinity. 
%Similar results hold in the case where for all $j$ $s_j$ is finite. We assume A3 holds to simplify the exposition and because we derive a new necessary condition on signal strength in that setting. Results in the finite case are discussed in the Appendix~\ref{sec:appendixGSMsfinite}.

\subsection{Gaussian sequence model and thresholding}\label{sec:GSMgen}

The Gaussian sequence model assumes:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:GSM}
	\bs y \;=\; \sqrt{n} \bs \beta^{\star} + \bs\epsilon,\qquad \bs \epsilon \sim N(0,\sigma^2 I_p),
\end{equation}
where $\bs y \in \mathbb{R}^p$ and without loss of generality we set $\sigma^2=1$ to simplify notation. It is a popular simplified model for high-dimensional inference, and is also central to non-parametric statistics. See Chapter 3 in \cite{johnstone} for a complete introduction. 
%The definition of section \ref{sec:intro_blockpen} for linear regression in \eqref{eq:Mhat} can be straightforwardly extended to the Gaussian sequence model. 
Let $\tilde{\bs \beta}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bs y \sim N(\bs \beta^*,\tfrac{1}{n}I_p)$ be the MLE under \eqref{eq:GSM}, and $\tilde{\bs \beta}_M$ the MLE under $M$ (with $j^{th}$ entry equal to $\tilde{\beta}_j$ if $j \in M$ and 0 otherwise).
%In the Gaussian sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, the MLE under the full model $V$ is $\tilde{\bs \beta}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\bs y$ and it is distributed $N(\bs \beta^*,\tfrac{1}{n}I_p)$. The set $\mathcal{M}$ can be taken to be power set of $V$ and for any $M\in\mathcal{M}$, the MLE $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}$ over $\mathcal{L}_M$ is equal to $\tilde{\bs\beta}_M$ (the restriction of $\tilde{\bs \beta}$ to entries in $M$). It follows a convenient characterization of $\hat{S}^b$:
\david{Lemma \ref{lem:useful-reformulation-GSM} can go into the supplement without harm, to save space and ease the paper's flow.}\piotr{I agree}
\begin{lem}\label{lem:useful-reformulation-GSM}
In the Gaussian sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, $\hat{S}^b$ defined in \eqref{eq:Mhat} satisfies:
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:MhatGSM}
  \hat S^b\;\in\;\arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \bigg\{\tfrac{1}{2}\| \sqrt{n}\tilde{\bs \beta}_M\|^2-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j|\bigg\}, \qquad M=M_1\cup \ldots\cup M_b.	
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

The sequence model generalizes orthogonal linear regression with normalized columns, i.e. when $\bs X$ in \eqref{eq:linearmodel} satisfies $\bs X^\top \bs X=n I_p$.
%variable selection in the Gaussian sequence model generalizes selection in orthogonal linear regression with normalized columns, that is when $\bs X$ in \eqref{eq:linearmodel} satisfies $\bs X^\top \bs X=n I_p$. 
Then, $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n}\bs X^{\top}\bs y \sim N(\bs \beta^*,\tfrac{1}{n}I_p)$, and selection with $\hat{S}^b$ operates as in the sequence model.\piotr{What is $\tilde{\bs\beta}'$? Also, for the record, I still cannot understand why you say that the GSM generalizes orthogonal regression not the other way around.} \paul{Some of the explanation has been commented out, maybe we need to add some back. $\tilde{\bs\beta}'$ is the MLE under the "full model" $V$ in orth linear regression. $\tilde{\bs \beta}$ is the MLE under the "full model" $V$ in GSM.} \paul{I think GSM generalizes orthogonal linear regression only from the the point of view of selection/selection with penalized methods? I think we said it before but GSM allows $n<p$ which is not possible with orth. linear regression. That's not enough for you?}\piotr{In GSM $n=p$ really, you just introduced a parameter, which you call $n$. But this is not a big point for me. David said that everybody in the literature agrees that GSM is more general and I see no point in fightting over this :) Please add back everything needed to understand the notation. }
%In that case, the MLE under the full model $V$ is $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n}\bs X^{\top}\bs y$, it is distributed $N(\bs \beta^*,\tfrac{1}{n}I_p)$ and for any $M\in\mathcal{M}$, the MLE $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}$ is $\tilde{\bs\beta}_M$.

%As a result, selection with $\hat{S}^b$ operates in the same way in the two settings and the discussions and results presented in this Section apply to both. 
%Orthogonal linear regression is however a more restrictive model since, unlike the Gaussian sequence model, it requires $n\geq p$.

% We first consider the case where the columns of $\bs X$ are orthogonal and normalized: $\bs X^\top \bs X=n I_p$. This case covers orthogonal designs in the linear regression \eqref{eq:linearmodel} and necessarily $p\leq n$. It also covers 
% In that model, the design matrix can be taken to be $\bs X=\sqrt{n}I_p$ and the number of samples is $p$. The quantity $n$ controls the shrinking rate of the MLE variance and does not constrain $p$. The Gaussian sequence model \piotr{Citing a book always requires a more precise citation (section number etc)}\paul{It is an unpublished book, but I cited it as a book and added the chapter number}. 

% Denote $\tilde{\bs \beta}=\frac{1}{n}\bs X^{\top}\bs y$. 

The next proposition shows that the block-wise $\ell_0$ penalty reduces to hard-thresholding $\tilde{\bs \beta}$, with block-dependent thresholds.
%From the characterization of Lemma~\ref{lem:useful-reformulation-GSM}, we can show that linear $\ell_0$-penalty approaches are exactly equivalent to hard thresholding $\tilde{\bs\beta}$.
\begin{prop}\label{prop:isthresh}
	In the Gaussian sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, $\hat{S}^b$ defined in \eqref{eq:Mhat} satisfies $\hat S^b=\hat S^b_1\cup \cdots \cup \hat S^b_b$, where, for each $j=1,\ldots,b$,
	$$
	\hat S_j^b\;:=\;\left\{i\in B_j:\; |\tilde{\beta}_i|> \sqrt{\frac{2\kappa_j}{n}}\right\}.
	$$ 
	% Each $\hat S^b_j$ is defined uniquely unless $|\tilde{\beta}_i|= \sqrt{\frac{2\kappa_j}{n}}$ for some $j=1,\ldots,b$ and $i\in B_j$.
 % \paul{I removed the discussion on whether $\hat S^b_j$ is defined uniquely. Because I define it as the smallest minimizer now. Also now $\hat S_j^b\;:=\;\left\{i\in B_j:\; |\tilde{\beta}_i|> \sqrt{\frac{2\kappa_j}{n}}\right\}$ and not $\hat S_j^b\;:=\;\left\{i\in B_j:\; |\tilde{\beta}_i|\geq \sqrt{\frac{2\kappa_j}{n}}\right\}$ anymore. It makes it more consistent with the $\ell_1$ selectors that have $>$ instead of $\geq$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:l1isthreshold} }
\end{prop}

%From Proposition~\ref{prop:isthresh} it follows that with standard linear $\ell_0$-penalties there is a single threshold while block informed linear $\ell_0$-penalty allow for different thresholds across blocks. 
Thus, equivalently, we study generic hard thresholding model selectors of the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thresholdS}
\hat S^{T,b}\;:=\;\{i\in B_j: |\tilde{\beta}_i|>\tau_j\}, \qquad\bs \tau=(\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_b)\in \R^b_{>0}.	
\end{equation}
\paul{I feel like we should highlight more this definition. Because we say in the introduction our object of study is $\hat S^b$ and then have an entire section on $\hat S^{T,b}$. Someone not reading very carefully could easily get lost. Maybe as we have special formats for Lemmas, Prop and theorem. We could have one for Definition to be used for selectors $\hat S^b$ and $\hat S^{T,b}$.}
%$\hat S^{T,b}$ generalizes standard hard thresholding. Taking $b=1$ gives the standard hard threshold selector. 
\paul{I think we could drop the 1 when we refer to the standard hard threshold selector $\hat S^{T,1}$ to make the notation lighter and more consistent with other notations in the discussion of the benefits.}
\david{Agreed. I added $\hat{S}$ to the lhs in \eqref{eq:stdl0} so that it's introduced upfront. Moreover, I would just use $\hat{S}^b$ instead of $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ to simplify notation, since $\hat{S}^{b}=\hat{S}^{T,b}$ in the sequence model. And then just $\hat{S}$ for the standard selector.}

Most popular variable selection procedures also take this block-based thresholding form, including for the LASSO and adaptive LASSO (Lemma \ref{lem:l1isthreshold}), Bayesian procedures under most standard priors \citep{davidomiros} where one sets a different prior inclusion probability in each block.

\david{I would end the section here, and move Lemma \ref{lem:l1isthreshold} to the supplement.}\piotr{I agree. Also, I do not know what happened to this lemma. Now the link to adaptive LASSO is not given explicitly and it is hard to decipher what is the meaning of the second part of this result.} \paul{Something that we lost in the trimming of text (a while ago) is the following argument that seemed important to me. When we say we do non-exchangeable penalties Adaptive LASSO comes to mind naturally. Initially we explained that adalasso with MLE / LASSO weights does not help with selection because it is eventually equivalent to having a single threshold.}\piotr{Perhaps, as David suggested, move this lemma to the appendix and add back all the necessary discussion there. }

%Results analogous to Proposition~\ref{prop:isthresh} hold for many other variable selection procedures. We now show it for $\ell_1$-penalized procedures. 
\begin{lem}\label{lem:l1isthreshold}
	In the sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, selecting the non-zero entries  of the solution to 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genl1}
	\underset{\bs \beta \in \R^p}{\rm minimize} \;\;\tfrac12\|\bs y-\sqrt{n}\bs\beta\|^2+\sum_{j=1}^b \lambda_j \sum_{i \in B_j}|\beta_{i}|
\end{equation}
for non-negative %penalty parameters 
$\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_b$  is equivalent to taking the selector $\hat S^{T,b}$ with $\bs \tau=(\lambda_1/n,\ldots,\lambda_b/n)$. Let $\bs\beta^\circ$ be either the MLE or the LASSO estimator with penalty $\lambda^\circ$. Selecting the non-zero entries  of the solution to the problem
\begin{equation}\label{eq:genl1ada}
	\underset{\bs \beta \in \R^p}{\rm minimize} \;\;\tfrac12\|\bs y-\sqrt{n}\bs\beta\|^2+\sum_{j=1}^b \lambda_j \sum_{i \in B_j}\tfrac{|\beta_{i}|}{|\beta_i^\circ|}.
\end{equation}
is equivalent to taking the selector $\hat S^{T,b}$ with $\bs \tau=(\sqrt{\lambda_1/n},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_b/n})$ if $\bs\beta^\circ$ is the MLE and with $\bs \tau=(\lambda^\circ/2n+\sqrt{(\lambda^\circ/2n)^2+\lambda_1/n},\ldots,\lambda^\circ/2n+\sqrt{(\lambda^\circ/2n)^2+\lambda_b/n})$ if $\bs\beta^\circ$ is a LASSO estimate.
\end{lem}

%Lemma~\ref{lem:l1isthreshold} introduces block informed versions of the $\ell_1$ penalties in LASSO \cite{lasso} and adaptive LASSO \cite{adalasso} that recover the original penalties when $b=1$. The lemma shows that in the sequence model the statistical properties of $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ extend to $\ell_1$ penalties too. 
%This result holds also in orthogonal linear regression replacing $ \tilde{\bs\beta}$ by $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{\prime}$.

%Although we do not discuss it in detail here, the block-thresholded selector $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ also includes Bayesian procedures. Under most standard priors Bayesian variable selection operates by hard thresholding $\tilde{\bs\beta}$ in the orthogonal case \citep{davidomiros}. One may set different prior inclusion probabilities in each block, and the highest posterior probability model results in block thresholding. 


\subsection{Selection based on block thresholds}
\label{ssec:block_thresholds}


%\paul{Added this outline of subsection } \david{great, thanks}
We now study the statistical performance of the block-based thresholding operator in \eqref{eq:thresholdS}.
% the model selector in \eqref{eq:Mhat}. By Proposition~\ref{prop:isthresh}, we equivalently study 
%model selector $\hat S^{T,b}$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:isthresh}, statistical properties of $\hat S^{T,b}$ apply to our selector of interest $\hat S^{b}$ in the Gaussian sequence model. 
Block thresholding was previously studied in the context of parameter estimation for wavelet-based models with equally sized blocks; see Chapters 7 to 9 in \cite{johnstone}. % for a complete discussion. 
We focus here on its variable selection properties. We successively analyze properties relative to false positives, false negatives, exact recovery and rates of convergence.  
%The relevant quantities are $p_j=|B_j|$ and $s_j=|S_j|$ for $j=1,\ldots,b$. 
\bigskip

\noindent\textbf{False positives.} 
%\noindent\textbf{False positives (guarantees for $\hat S^{T,b}\subseteq S$).} 
A false positive %in a block threshold selector 
occurs when there exists $j=1,\ldots,b$ and $i\in B_j$ such that $\beta^\star_i=0$ %(equiv. $i\in B_j\setminus S_j$) 
but $|\tilde \beta_i|>\tau_j$. In other words, $\hat S^{T,b}\not\subseteq S$. A sufficient condition to guarantee that asymptotically $\hat S^{T,b}\subseteq S$ is:
\begin{enumerate}[start=4,label={(A\arabic*)}]
    \item For all $j=1,\ldots,b$ and all sufficiently large $n$
\begin{equation*}\label{cond:suffIallblockthresh} 
\tau_j \;\geq\;  \sqrt{\frac{{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}}{{n}}},	
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
The next proposition shows that %in addition to being sufficient 
A4 is also essentially necessary.
\piotr{edited the proposition}
\begin{prop}\label{prop:suffnecIblockthresh}In the %Gaussian
sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, assume A1 and A2.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item [a)] If A4 holds then % $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ satisfies:
$P(\hat{S}^{T,b}\subseteq S) \to 1$.
\item [b)] If for some $j\in\{1,\ldots,b\}$,
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIallblockthresh0}
\lim_{n\to \infty} \frac{\tau_j}{\sqrt{\frac{{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}}{{n}}}} \;<\;1\; ,	\end{equation}
then  $P(\hat{S}^{T,b}\subseteq S ) \not \to 1$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{prop}
Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh} shows that %assumption~A4 is  sufficient and that 
a necessary condition to have, asymptotically, no false positives %and model recovery 
is: 
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIallblockthresh}
\lim_{n\to \infty} \frac{\tau_j}{\sqrt{\tfrac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}}}\;\geq\;1\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.	
\end{equation}
Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh} extends to $\hat{S}^{T, b}$ results known for $\hat{S}^{T, 1}$ in orthogonal linear regression \cite{ slope, buhlmannvandegeerbook, wainwrighthighdimbook} and in the Gaussian sequence model in \cite{ariascastro} and Chapter 3 of \cite{johnstone}.
\bigskip

\noindent\textbf{False negatives.} 
%\noindent\textbf{False negatives (guarantees for $S\subseteq \hat S^{T,b}$).} 
False negatives occur when $S\not\subseteq \hat S^{T,b}$, i.e. for some $j \in \{1,\ldots,b\}$ and $i\in B_j$,  we have that $\beta^\star_i\neq 0$ and $|\tilde\beta_i|\leq \tau_j$. %This means that $\tau_j \geq \min_{i \in S_j}|y_i/\sqrt{n}|$ for some $j=1,\ldots, b$. 
Control of false negatives in a block $B_j$ requires bounding the minimum of $|S_j|=s_j$ independent Gaussians with non-zero means $\beta^{\star}_i$, $i\in S_j$. This can be achieved by setting a betamin condition %, that is a condition on the smallest non-zero parameter 
on $\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}\;:=\;\min\{|\beta^\star_i|: i\in S_j\}$,
and a condition on the thresholds $\tau_j$ for $j=1,\ldots,b$.  
Intuitively, to have no false positives (also known as screening property), each $\tau_j$ needs to be smaller than $\beta^*_{\min,j}$.
%It is clear that, to have that $S\subseteq \hat S^{T,b}$, also known as screening property, each $\tau_j$ cannot be too large; in particular $\tau_j$ needs to be smaller than $\beta^*_{\min,j}$. 
A closely-related sufficient condition is: 
\begin{enumerate}[start=5,label={(A\arabic*)}]
    \item For all $j=1,\ldots,b$ and sufficiently large $n$
\begin{equation*}\label{cond:suffIIallblockthresh}
	 \beta_{\min,j}^{\star} - \tau_j \;\geq\; \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}},
%	 \beta_{\min,j}^{\star}-\tau_j \geq \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}},
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}

\begin{prop}\label{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh}
 In the sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, assume A1-A3 and A5. Then %$\hat{S}^{T,b}$ satisfies:
%\begin{equation}\label{cond:suffIIallblockthresh}
%	\lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}-\tau_j}{\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}}} \;>\;1 ,
%\end{equation}
$P(S\subseteq \hat{S}^{T,b} ) \to 1$.
\end{prop}
Condition A5 for standard thresholding was proved to be sufficient for selection consistency in orthogonal linear regression in \cite{wainwrighthighdimbook}. Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh} generalizes it to block thresholds. The study of {\it necessary} conditions %for $S\subseteq \hat S^{T,b}$ asymptotically
is more complicated. We provide below a first general condition valid for any vector $\bs \beta^*$ and a second tight condition for a worst case where $\beta_i^*=\beta_{\min}^*$ for all $i\in S$.
\piotr{edited the proposition. also in the whole paper I replaced $min$ with $\min$ for consistency}
\begin{prop}\label{prop:necIIblockthresh}In the %Gaussian
sequence model \eqref{eq:GSM}, assume A1 and A2.
\begin{enumerate}
    \item [a)] Suppose that $\tau_j<\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ satisfies \eqref{cond:necIallblockthresh} for some $j \in \{1,\ldots,b\}$. If \begin{equation}\label{cond:necIIallblockthresh2}
    \lim_{n\to \infty} \sqrt{n}(\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}-\tau_j)\; <\; \infty
\end{equation}
then $P(S\subseteq \hat{S}^{T,b} ) \not \to 1$.
\item [b)] Suppose there exists $j\in \{1,\ldots,b\}$ such that  $\beta^{\star}_i=\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}$ for all $i \in S_j$, $\tau_j<\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ satisfies \eqref{cond:necIallblockthresh}, and  $s_j/p_j<1$. If A3 holds and, in addiiton, 
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIIallblockthresh}
\lim_{n\to \infty} 	\frac{\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}-\tau_j}{\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{\ln(s_j)}{n}}} \;\leq\;  1\end{equation}
then $P(S\subseteq \hat{S}^{T,b} ) \not \to 1$.
\end{enumerate}\end{prop}
\cite{abraham2023sharp} showed the necessity of \eqref{cond:necIIallblockthresh2} for consistency in the single-block case %in the sequence model 
in more restrictive asymptotic regimes satisfying $p/s \to \infty$. %Proposition~\ref{prop:necIIblockthresh}a) shows it generalizes to $b>1$ and in a larger class of regimes. 
Proposition~\ref{prop:necIIblockthresh}b) gives a new precise necessary  rate of growth of $\sqrt{n}\left(\beta_{\min}^{\star}-\tau\right)$ for a worst case in which all signals are equal. It shows that in that case sufficient assumption~A5 is essentially necessary up to a constant factor, and that A5 cannot be relaxed in general.
\bigskip

\noindent\textbf{Exact model recovery guarantees.} 
Combining Conditions A4 (Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh}) and A5 (Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh}),
we asymptotically recover the correct support if for all $j=1,\ldots,b$,
%To guarantee model recovery %with high probability the block thresholds $\tau_j$ must be $\geq \sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)/n}$ %greater or equal to  $\sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)/n}$ 
%(assumption~A4, Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh}), and $\leq {\beta^\star_{\min,2}}+\sqrt{2\ln(s_j)/n}$ (assumption~A5, Proposition~\ref{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh}). In particular, we get asymptotic guarantees for correct support recovery as long as for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, $\tau_j$ and $\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}$ satisfy both A4 and A5 that is, for all $n$ sufficiently large:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:thetamin}
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}}\;\;\leq\;\;\tau_j\;\;\leq\;\;\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}}
\end{equation}
for sufficiently large $n$. This implies that %A4 and A5 then imply 
that for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, %for all $n$ sufficiently large:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betamin}
    {\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j \geq \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}}
\end{equation}
By Propositions~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh} and~\ref{prop:suffnecIIblockthresh} signals as small as the right-hand side in \eqref{eq:betamin} can be recovered in block $B_j$. As we analyze the benefits of block-based thresholding, %based on external information, 
we will discuss signal recoverable with $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ and $\hat{S}^{T,1}$ and will refer to betamin condition \eqref{eq:betamin} as the \textbf{minimal betamin condition}. \paul{David does not like this expression? Maybe "smallest signal recoverable"? Although we show it is sharp only up to constant factor and in a particular worst case} \david{Maybe refer to it as "blockwise betamin condition? "As we analyze the benefits of block-based thresholding, we will refer to \eqref{eq:betamin} as the block-wise betamin condition".}\piotr{i like ``blockwise betamin condition''}

%Conversely for betamin smaller than the right-hand side in \eqref{eq:betamin}, there is no choice of thresholds $(\tau_1,\ldots,\tau_b)$ for which selection consistency is guaranteed. 
Propositions~\ref{prop:suffnecIblockthresh} b) and \ref{prop:necIIblockthresh} show that Conditions A4-A5 are essentially necessary. It follows that betamin condition \eqref{eq:betamin} is near-necessary for selection consistency in the following sense.
\piotr{Can we have the same numbering for everybody? Now lemmas have different numbering than propositions, which makes harder to look for results.}
\begin{lem}\label{lem:necbetaminblockthresh}
    If there exists $j\in\{1,\ldots,b\}$ such that $\beta^{\star}_i=\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}$ for all $i \in S_j$, $s_j/p_j<1$ and
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:necbetaminblockthresh}
      \lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\beta_{\min,j}^*}{\sqrt{\tfrac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}}+\sqrt{\tfrac{\pi}{2}\tfrac{\ln(s_j)}{n}}}\;<\; 1  
    \end{equation}
then under Conditions A1-A3,\;  $P(\hat{S}^{T,b} = S)\not \to 1$
\end{lem}
Lemma~\ref{lem:necbetaminblockthresh} shows the minimal betamin condition \eqref{eq:betamin} cannot be relaxed much in general. Under the conditions of Lemma~\ref{lem:necbetaminblockthresh}, if $\beta_{\min,j}^*$ remains strictly smaller than $\sqrt{2\ln(p_j-s_j)/n}+\sqrt{\pi\ln(s_j)/2n}$, selection consistency is not possible. \paul{David suggested to delete this last sentence, I don't undertand why. It seems correct and important to me} \david{It's important but it seems repetitive, we already stated this just before the lemma.}\piotr{Better to remove the part before the lemma and move it here. Placing the conclusion in the end strenghtens the message. (but I am fine either way)}


\bigskip

\noindent\textbf{Rate of convergence.} From the %proofs of the 
preceding results, we can bound the rate of convergence of $P(\hat S^{T, b}\neq S)$.\piotr{Here we do not assume neither A1 nor A2? also I slightly edited this result so check if you agree} %We also give oracle threshold values $\tau^\star_j$ that approximately minimize the bound, and the resulting oracle rate.
\begin{thm}\label{theo:fullrecovGS}
Assume A4 and A5. Then, for sufficiently large $n$,
%	Assume that the thresholds $\tau_j$ and the $\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ satisfy A4 and A5 then for all sufficiently large $n$
\begin{equation} \label{eq:rateconvgsm}
P(\hat{S}^{T,b} \neq S)\;\leq\;\sum_{j=1}^b\,\,\frac{e^{-\tfrac{n}{2}\big[\tau_j^2-\tfrac{2\ln(p_j-s_j)}{n}\big]}}{\sqrt{\pi\ln(p_j-s_j)}}+\frac{e^{-\tfrac{n}{2}\big[(\beta^\star_{\min,j}-\tau_j)^2-\tfrac{2\ln(s_j)}{n}\big]}}{\sqrt{\pi\ln(s_j)}}.
\end{equation}
On the other hand, if $\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ satisfy \eqref{eq:betamin} for every block $j$, and the thresholds $\tau_j$ take oracle values
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:optimtauj}
\tau^\star_j \;=\; \frac{1}{2}\beta^\star_{\min,j}+\frac{1}{n\beta^\star_{\min,j}}(\ln({p_j-s_j})-\ln({s_j})),
\end{equation}
then A4 and A5 hold and, for sufficiently large $n$, %there exists $n_0$ such that for all $n\geq n_0$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:optimrateconvgsm}
    P(\hat{S}^{T,b} \neq S) \; \leq \; 2 \sum_{j=1}^be^{-[\frac{n}{8}{\beta^\star_{\min,j}}^2-\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j),\ln(s_j)\}]}
\end{equation}
\end{thm}
In \eqref{eq:rateconvgsm} the first component of each term in the sum decreases exponentially in $n\tau^2/2$, while the second component decreases exponentially in $n(\beta_{\min,j}^*-\tau)^2/2$. The choice $\tau^*_j$ ensures that both terms are equal and hence approximates the values minimizing \eqref{eq:rateconvgsm}.  \piotr{Good moment to explain very briefly why we call them ``oracle''.} 
%For such oracle threshold, we obtain an oracle rate in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} that is driven by the distances between ${\beta^\star_{\min,j}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!^{2}\;\;\;$ and $\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j),\ln(s_j)\}$. 
The resulting bound in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} is tightest in the worst-case scenario where $\beta_i^*=\beta_{\min}^*$ for all $i \in S$, and approximates the fastest rate achievable in that worst case.
%That bound is valid for all vectors $\bs\beta^*$ and is thus the tightest for the worst case in which $\beta_i^*=\beta_{\min}^*$ for all $i \in S$. The oracle rate in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} approximates the fastest rate achievable in that worst case.


\subsection{Gains and losses of block thresholding}\label{sec:prosconsblock}

%\paul{added this outline}
We now examine the benefits brought by block penalization. %thresholding based on externally defined blocks. 
We start with a simple example to build intuition and then discuss %go on with discussing separately 
two types of benefit: those relative to conditions for selection consistency and those relative to rates of convergence.
\bigskip

Intuitively, block penalties allow adapting 
%Broadly speaking, block penalization provides the possibility to adapt 
to varying sparsity $s_j$  or signal strengths $\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ across blocks. 
When $p_j-s_j$ is small one may use smaller thresholds $\tau_j$ and still control false positives, and when $\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ is large one may use larger $\tau_j$ and still control false negatives.
As the blocks become more
%As the external information driving the blocks becomes more 
discriminative, that is, more distinct $p_j-s_j$ and $\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ across blocks, the larger the potential benefits.
Consider a somewhat extreme example where $n = 1$, $p = 100$, $s = 10$ and $\beta_i^\star=4$ for all $i\in S$. There are $b=2$ blocks: a large sparse block with $s_1=0$, $p_1-s_1=80$ and a smaller non-sparse block with $s_2=10$, $p_2-s_2=10$. In the left panel of Figure~\ref{fig:starter}, a standard threshold selector is used with $\tau=\sqrt{2 \ln(p-s)/ n}$, the minimal threshold such that the FWER vanishes (dashed line). The large number of truly zero parameters $p-s$ forces a high threshold, which leads to a false negative. In the right panel, block thresholds are set to $\tau_j=\sqrt{2 \ln(p_j-s_j)/ n}$.
Adjusting $\tau_2$ to the lower number of truly zero means in block 2 ($p_2-s_2$) allows recovering the true model. 
\begin{figure}[t]
\includegraphics[scale=.6]{images/starter.example.pdf}
\centering
\caption{In both panels, the 90 white dots are generated from $N(0, 1)$ and the 10 black dots are generated from $N(4, 1)$. Left panel: the dashed line is $\sqrt{2 \ln (p-s)/n}$, the minimal threshold such that FWER vanishes. Right panel: observations are grouped in two blocks. The dashed lines are the minimal block thresholds for the vanishing of the FWER} %with $\hat{S}^{T,b}$.

\david{I moved this paragraph that you introduced later (in the examples) to here. To me, this captures the essence of why block thresholding improves upon global thresholding. I also placed the corollary immediately afterwards, to connect it with the general principle.}

%\noindent\textbf{Thresholds for $\hat{S}^{T,1}$ and $\hat{S}^{T,b}$.}
More precisely, Conditions A4-A5 give ranges of threshold values that are essentially necessary and sufficient for asymptotic support recovery. For the standard threshold selector $\hat{S}^{T,1}$, the range for the single threshold $\tau$ is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rangethreshex}
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}}\;\;\leq\;\;\tau\;\;\leq\;\;\beta^{\star}_{\min}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s)}{n}}.	
\end{equation}
For a block threshold selector $\hat{S}^{T,b}$, in the $b=2$ block case the ranges for $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$ are
\begin{equation}
    \begin{aligned}\label{eq:rangeblockthreshex}
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_1-s_1)}{n}}\;\;\leq\;\;\tau_1\;\;\leq\;\;\beta^{\star}_{\min,1}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_1)}{n}}\\
  \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_2-s_2)}{n}}\;\;\leq\;\;\tau_2\;\;\leq\;\;\beta^{\star}_{\min}-\sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_2)}{n}}        
    \end{aligned}
\end{equation}

\color{blue}
If the range for $\tau_1$ does not overlap with that for $\tau_2$, 
which, for example, occurs if $\beta_{\min,1}^{\star} - \sqrt{(2/n) \ln(s_1)} < \sqrt{(2/n) \ln(p_2-s_2)}$,
then a global threshold $\tau$ cannot possibly satisfy both conditions in 
\eqref{eq:rangeblockthreshex} and consistent model recovery is not possible.
We next give a precise instance where this occurs.
\color{black}\piotr{nice!}
%The betamin condition \eqref{eq:necbetaminblockthresh} in Lemma~\ref{lem:necbetaminblockthresh} allows to describe settings where consistent model recovery is possible for block thresholding, but not for standard thresholding.
%The necessary betamin condition of Lemma~\ref{lem:necbetaminblockthresh} also allows to precisely describe settings where block selection makes possible model recovery. 
\david{Corollary 3.1 is similar to my added discussion in blue above, but if features $(s,p)$ instead of $(s_j,p_j)$. Is it a refined version? How does it connect to the previous discussion? I just wrote "a precise instance" for lack of a better way how to set up the result.}

\begin{cor}\label{cor:selconstimpossible}
Assume A1-A3, that $s/p<1$, and $\beta^{\star}_i=\beta_{\min}^{\star}$ for all $i \in S$. If
\begin{align*}
    &\lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\beta_{\min}^*}{\sqrt{\tfrac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}}+\sqrt{\tfrac{\pi}{2}\tfrac{\ln(s)}{n}}}\;<\; 1 
\end{align*}
and for $j=1,\ldots,b$, $(\tau_j,\beta^\star_{\min,j})$ satisfy A4-A5 \piotr{Cannot we just assume A1-A5 holds in the beginning?},
then %under assumption A1 to A3 
$P(\hat{S}^{T,1} = S)\not \to 1$ and $P(\hat{S}^{T,b} = S)\to 1$.
\end{cor}% We compared here the minimal betamin conditions, assuming thresholds $\tau$ and $\tau_j$'s set at the smallest value guaranteeing the vanishing of the FWER. The magnitude of gain in practice will depend on the values chosen for $\tau$ and $\tau_j$ where important considerations like guarantees on rate of convergence and that $s$ and $s_j$ are a priori unknown come into play. 
\paul{Do I need to have proof or can we say it is immediate?} \david{I normally err on the cautious side and add a brief proof where I say "The result follows immediately from..."}\piotr{If you call it ``Corollary'' perhaps fine to skip the proof. But you can write a sentence below just to guide readers.}

\noindent\textbf{Minimal betamin conditions.} The possibility to lower thresholds in the less sparse blocks as in the example above make betamin conditions for selection consistency milder with block thresholding.
%independently of the discriminative power of the external information
To simplify the exposition and without loss of generality, assume the smallest $|\beta_i^*|$ for all $i\in S$, $\beta_{\min}^{\star}$, is located in block $b$. 
From \eqref{eq:betamin}, we obtain the minimal betamin condition for the standard threshold selector $\hat{S}^{1}$:
\david{Maybe just say betamin, for simplicity?}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betamincon.suffthresh}
    \beta_{\min}^{\star} \;>\; \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\ln(s)}{n}}
\end{equation} 
For the block threshold selector $\hat{S}^{T,b}$, since $\beta_{\min}^{\star}$ is located in block $b$, condition \eqref{eq:betamin} requires:
\david{Why is it enough to look only at block $b$ ? I mean, even if $\beta_{\min}^\star$ is in block $b$, $(p_j,s_j)$ from other blocks could be such that \eqref{eq:betamincon.suffblockthresh} holds in block $b$, but not for some other block $j$ no?}\piotr{Yes, this should be clarified.}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betamincon.suffblockthresh}
    \beta_{\min}^{\star} \;>\; \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_b-s_b)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\ln(s_b)}{n}}
\end{equation} 
Denote the right hand-side in inequalities above: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betaminbardef}
    \underline{\beta}_{\min}^{*} := \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p-s)}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s)}{n}},\qquad
    \underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,b} := \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(p_b-s_b)}{n}} + \sqrt{\frac{2\ln(s_b)}{n}}
\end{equation} Since $p_b-s_b \leq p - s$ and $s_b \leq s$, $\underline{\beta}^{*}_{\min,b} \leq \underline{\beta}_{\min}^{*}$ and condition \eqref{eq:betamincon.suffblockthresh} is always milder than \eqref{eq:betamincon.suffthresh}. Selector $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ is then able to recover smaller signals than $\hat{S}^{T,1}$. The magnitude of the difference depends on how $p-s$ and $s$ compare to $p_b-s_b$ and $s_b$. The gain thus depends on whether the block structure induced by the external information is indicative of varying sparsity. \david{I didn't find sub-section "Minimal betamin conditions" super-informative (given what we already said earlier). This is however used later for Figure Figure~\ref{fig:ratiobetamincond}, so maybe we can introduce $\underline{\beta}_{\min}^{*}$ later, when presenting that example.}\piotr{agreed}
%The analysis extends to the blocks $B_j\neq B_b$  and in general in every block, the strictness of the requirements on the smallest signal depends on how $p-s$ and $s$ compare to $p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$.  To illustrate this, we compare the growth rates required in \eqref{eq:betamincon.suffblockthresh} and in \eqref{eq:betamincon.suffthresh} in a few examples. 

\bigskip

\noindent\textbf{Rates of convergence.}   We just saw that block thresholding can attain consistent variable selection where standard thresholding cannot. We now discuss differences in the probability of correct variable selection, when consistent selection is possible for both procedures. We assume the block thresholds %in $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ 
are set to their oracle values $\tau_j^\star$ %in \eqref{eq:optimtauj} 
and similarly the single threshold %in $\hat{S}^{T,1}$  
takes its oracle value $\tau^\star$.
Let ${OR}^b_{orth}$ be the oracle convergence rate for $\tau_j^\star$ %$\hat{S}^{T,b}$ 
in \eqref{eq:optimrateconvgsm} and ${OR}_{orth}$ that for $\tau^\star$. %$\hat{S}^{T,1}$.
%\paul{David suggested to replace "oracle rate" by "upper-bound on the probability of not selecting S" but we have referred to this quantity as oracle rate before. It not any upper bound, it is more or less the smallest upper bound in the worst case} \david{ok}
% \begin{align*}
%     &{OR}^b_{orth}= 2 \sum_{j=1}^be^{-[\frac{n}{8}{\beta^\star_{\min,j}}^2-\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j),\ln(s_j)\}]}\\
%     &{OR}_{orth} = 2 e^{-[\frac{n}{8}{\beta_{\min}^\star}^2-\max\{\ln(p-s),\ln(s)\}]}
% \end{align*}
Then
%The ratio of the two quantities is:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ratioCR}
    \frac{{OR}^b_{orth}}{{OR}_{orth}}\;=\;\sum_{j=1}^be^{-\frac{n}{8}\left({\beta^\star_{\min,j}}^2-{\beta_{\min}^\star}^2\right)+\max\{\ln(p-s),\ln(s)\}-\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j),\ln(s_j)\}}
\end{equation}
Since $\beta_{\min,j}^\star \geq \beta_{\min}^\star$, Equation \eqref{eq:ratioCR} shows that for $n$ large enough ${OR}^b_{orth} \leq {OR}_{orth}$ for any partition in blocks, i.e. the convergence rate for $\tau_j^\star$ is never worse than for $\tau^\star$. \david{As currently written, when $\beta_{\min,j}^\star = \beta_{\min}^\star$ for all $j$, then \eqref{eq:ratioCR} doesn't vanish. Also, ${\beta^\star_{\min,j}}^2-{\beta_{\min}^\star}^2$ must be larger than the difference between the max's no? In \eqref{eq:ratioCR} should it be $-\max\{\ln(p-s),\ln(s)\}+\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j)$? I ask because the display and discussion below suggest that.}
%For oracle threshold values the guarantee on the convergence rate of $\hat{S}^{T, b}$ is always better than or equal to that of $\hat{S}^{T, 1}$. 
The magnitude of the gain depends on the discriminative power of the blocks. %external information. 
Consider a mildly sparse setting where $s<p-s$ and $s_j<p_j-s_j$ for every $j$. Then, assuming without loss of generality that $\beta_{\min,b}^\star=\beta_{\min}^\star$, we have
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{{OR}^b_{orth}}{{OR}_{orth}}\;=\; \frac{p_b-s_b}{p-s} \,+\,\sum_{j<b}\frac{p_j-s_j}{p-s}e^{-\frac{n}{8}\left({\beta^\star_{\min,j}}^2-{\beta_{\min}^\star}^2\right)}
\end{equation*}

There are then two sources of improvement in convergence rates: in any block $j$, a smaller number of truly zero means, that is $p_j-s_j\leq p-s$, or a larger smallest signal, $\beta^\star_{\min,j} > \beta_{\min}^\star$. Depending on the sparsity of the block, one of the two effects usually prevails. In blocks where $\beta^\star_{\min,j} \approx \beta_{\min}^\star$, improvement comes mainly from having a smaller number of truly zero means. In those blocks
one sets $\tau^\star_j < \tau^\star$,
%smaller $\tau^\star_j$ relative to $\tau^\star$,
%the smaller $p_j-s_j$ is compared to $p-s$, the smaller $\tau^\star_j$ is compared to $\tau^\star$
so that one may detect smaller signals. 
If %In blocks where 
$\beta^\star_{\min,j} \gg \beta_{\min}^\star$,
one sets $\tau^\star_j > \tau^\star$
%one sets larger $\tau^\star_j$ relative to $\tau^\star$, 
and the probability of false positives is reduced.
%In blocks where $\beta^\star_{\min,j} \gg \beta_{\min}^\star$, the improvement can be large even if $p_j-s_j$ is only marginally smaller that $p-s$. The improvement then mostly stems from $\tau^\star_j$ being larger than $\tau^\star$ and the probability of false positives being thus reduced. 
%Similarly, if in some blocks $\ln(s_j) > \ln(p_j-s_j)$, the gain in those blocks depends on the magnitude of $s_j$ versus $p-s$.

On the contrary, there is no improvement %in oracle convergence rate 
(${OR}^b_{orth}={OR}_{orth}$), %in the sense that ${OR}^b_{orth}={OR}_{orth}$, 
when for example %in the general sparse setting where 
we have a non-sparse setting where $s_j>p_j-s_j$, with
$p_j-s_j=(p-s)/b$ and $\beta^\star_{\min,j}=\beta_{\min}^\star$ for all $j$. In such case, the external information fails to identify blocks that are substantially differently sparse. 

\subsection{Examples with two blocks}\label{sec:examples}

We illustrate our results with concrete examples contemplating different sparsity regimes and informativeness of the blocks. %scenarios of external information. 
We focus on a mildly sparse setting where $\ln(s)=o(\ln(p-s))$, with two blocks ($b=2$). We assume that $\beta_{\min}^{\star}$, the smallest non-zero $|\beta_i^*|$ %for all $i\in S$ 
is located in block $2$. We start by plotting the range of valid thresholds for $\hat{S}^{T,1}$ and $\hat{S}^{T,b}$ as given in conditions A4 and A5. We then go on with comparing the minimal betamin conditions and the %end with a comparison of 
oracle convergence rates.

In all scenarios we set $s=e^{\sqrt{\ln(n)}}$ and, for simplicity, $s_1=s_2=s/2$. The difference in sparsity between blocks %block 2 and $V$ 
across scenarios varies as follows:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Scenario 1: $p-s=n$ and $p_2-s_2=n/2$. Here $\bs\beta^*$ is mildly sparse ($s/(p-s)= e^{\sqrt{\ln n} - \ln(n)}$) and the blocks are non-discriminative (both have the same number of truly 0 means $p_1-s_1=p_2-s_2$). %splitting $V$ in two blocks with the same number of truly 0 means.
    \item Scenario 2: $p-s=n^2$ and $p_2-s_2=n$. Here $\bs\beta^*$ is moderately sparse ($s/(p-s)= e^{\sqrt{\ln n} - 2\ln(n)}$) and the blocks are %external information is 
    discriminative ($(p_1-s_1)/(p_2-s_2)$ is a power of $n$). %with the ratio of number of truly 0 means between the two blocks being a power of $n$.
    \item Scenario 3: $p-s=e^{n/10}$ and $p_2-s_2=n^2$. Here $\bs\beta^*$ is highly sparse ($s/(p-s)= e^{\sqrt{\ln n} - n/10}$) and the blocks are %external information is 
    discriminative ($(p_1-s_1)/(p_2-s_2)$ is a power of $n$). %with the ratio of number of truly 0 means between the two blocks being exponential in $n$.
    \item Scenario 4: $p-s=n$ and $p_2-s_2=\ln(n)$. Here $\bs\beta^*$ is mildly sparse ($s/(p-s)= e^{\sqrt{\ln n} - \ln(n)}$) and the blocks are %external information is 
    highly discriminative (block 2 is non-sparse, since $\ln(p_2-s_2)=o(\ln(s_2))$).
    %with $\bs \beta^*_{B_2}$ being non sparse in the sense that $\ln(p_2-s_2)=o(\ln(s_2))$.
\end{itemize}

\section{High-dimensional linear regression}
%\section{Properties in high-dimensional linear regression}
\label{sec:properties_legression}

In Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} we showed that, in the sequence model, using block penalties allows consistent model recovery in settings where it is otherwise not possible (e.g. smaller signals), and improves oracle consistency rates. We now extend the results to linear regression. 
%Results of Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} show that in the Gaussian sequence model letting the penalization depend on external information allows the recovery of smaller signals. It also improves consistency rates under oracle penalty values. In this section we come back to the analysis of the general linear regression model \eqref{eq:linearmodel}.\paul{added this outline}
We present sufficient conditions, %minimal 
betamin conditions and rates for the probability of correct selection for the block informed $\ell_0$-penalized selector $\hat S^b$ in \eqref{eq:Mhat}. We also compare the properties of $\hat S^b$ to those of the standard $\ell_0$ selector $\hat S^1$. % in this linear regression setting.  
Whereas the framework is more involved than in the sequence model and the required proof techniques are different, the results
%The section is mathematically more involved but results 
remain conceptually the same. 

Here %In this section 
$V$ is the set of all variables and %for any $j$, 
$p_j-s_j$ and $s_j$ are respectively the numbers of inactive and active variables in block $B_j$. We require Assumptions A1-A2, but the number of active variables $s_j$ may either be fixed or go to infinity. %the $s_j$'s, being fixed or going to infinity. 
That is, we do not assume A3 anymore. For simplicity, we assume that $\hat{S}^b$ is unique and that one constrains attention to the set of models $\mathcal{M}$,
such that %the set of subsets $M\subset V$ such that 
$\bs X_M$ has full column rank for any $M \subset \mathcal{M}$, and that the data-generating true support $S$ lies $\mathcal{M}$ \paul{Should that be stated as assumptions "AX")} \david{Maybe not strictly necessary. Piotr, what do you think?}\piotr{Given that we say that it is not a crucial assumption (there is a way to get around it), I would not list it as a separate assumption and just leave it as it is.}. Non-uniqueness of $\hat{S}^b$ and non-full rank models can be accommodated though, at the expense of a slightly more involved treatment.

%\paul{added this outline} \david{ok}
Our analysis relies on a classical connection
%Our derivation of the properties of $\hat{S}^{b}$ here relies on the connection 
between $\ell_0$ penalties and Bayesian variable selection. 
Section \ref{ssec:regression_proof_strategy} reviews this connection and the proof strategy for our results.
The technical nature of the proof precludes a detailed exposition, we instead give only the most important ideas.
%We start by clarifying this connection and then introduce quantities that are key to our results and proofs. 
In Section \ref{ssec:regression_mainresult} we state our main theorem
%We continue with the statement of the main theorem 
giving variable selection consistency of $\hat{S}^{b}$, and we also give betamin conditions and oracle convergence rates as we did for the sequence model.
%Detailed proofs are gathered at the end of the paper.

Section \ref{ssec:regression_gains} discusses the gains of block penalization in further detail.


\subsection{Proof strategy}
\label{ssec:regression_proof_strategy}


We state a well-known %and convenient 
reformulation of $\hat S^b$ in linear regression \eqref{eq:linearmodel}. % follows from the explicit form of the MLE over each submodel $\mathcal{L}_M$. 
\begin{lem}\label{lem:useful-reformulation} Let $\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}= (\bs X_{\!\!M}^\top \bs X_{\!\!M})^{-1}\bs X_{\!\!M}^\top\bs y \in \R^{|M|}$ be the MLE under model $M\in \mathcal{M}$. Then $\hat S^b\;\in\;\arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} {\rm C}(M)$, where $M=M_1\cup \ldots\cup M_b$ and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CM}
    {\rm C}(M)\;: =\;\tfrac{1}{2}\|\bs X_M\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j|,
\end{equation}
%in linear regression model \eqref{eq:linearmodel}, %that is, 
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:MMLE}
%	\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}\;=\;(\bs X_{\!\!M}^\top \bs X_{\!\!M})^{-1}\bs X_{\!\!M}^\top\bs y.	
%\end{equation}
%Then $\hat S^b$ satisfies:
%	\begin{equation}\label{eq:Mhat2}
%  \hat S^b\;\in\;\arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \bigg\{\tfrac{1}{2}\|\bs X_{\!\!M}\tilde{\bs \beta}^{(M)}\|^2-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j|\bigg\}, \qquad M=M_1\cup \ldots\cup M_b.	
%\end{equation} 
\end{lem}
%The reformulation of 
By Lemma~\ref{lem:useful-reformulation}, $\hat S^b$ selects %proceeds by selecting the model 
$M\in \mathcal{M}$ with the largest score $C(M)$, or equivalently the largest normalized score
%Equivalently, $\hat S^b$ retains the model with the largest normalized score
\begin{equation}\label{eq:normalizedscore}
    NC(M)\;:=\;\frac{e^{{\rm C}(M)}}{\underset{M' \in \mathcal{M}}{\sum} e^{{\rm C}(M')}}.
\end{equation}
This normalized score can be understood as a pseudo-posterior probability for model $M$ in a Bayesian variable selection framework \citep{BIC}. \paul{We used "citep" instead "cite" here. is that for a specific reason?}
Proposition 1 in \cite{rossell2022concentration}, reproduced below as Lemma \ref{lem:L0toL1convergence}, proves that the expectation of such posterior probabilities bound
%As a result, normalized scores can be used to bound 
the probability of an incorrect variable selection $(\hat S^b\neq S)$. 

\begin{lem}\label{lem:L0toL1convergence} %Assume the linear model in \eqref{eq:linearmodel} with the block informed $\ell_0$-penalized selector $\hat S^b$ in \eqref{eq:Mhat}. Then
\begin{longlist}
\item 
$P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S) \,\,\leq \,\, 2 \sum_{M \in \mathcal{M}\setminus \{S\}} \E\left(NC(M)\right).$
\item  For any $M,M'\in\mathcal{M}$, such that $M\neq M'$, 
$NC(M)\;\leq \;\big(1+e^{C(M')-C(M)}\big)^{-1}$.
\end{longlist}
\end{lem}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence}(i) the vanishing, in expectation, of the sum of the normalized scores %$NC$ over models $M\in \mathcal M\setminus \{S\}$ 
guarantees selection consistency. Such vanishing is often referred to as the \textit{strong selection consistency} property \citep{narisettyhe2014}, requiring that pseudo-posterior model probabilities concentrate on the truth (except that here we prove $L_1$ convergence instead of convergence in probability).
To bound $\E(NC(M))$, by taking $M'=S$ in Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence} (ii), we may instead bound a simple function of $C(S)-C(M)$, the pairwise comparison of criteria values of $S$ and $M$. 
This is achieved by noting that $C(S)-C(M)=\tfrac12 L_{SM}+\Delta_{MS}$, where for any two models $M,T\subseteq V$, we denote
%To bound this sum, we upper-bound %derive an upper bound on 
%$\E(NC(M))$. %for $M\in \mathcal M\setminus \{S\}$. 
%Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence} allows us to do it by lower bounding $C(S)-C(M)$. 
%For any $M\subseteq V$ denote the block $\ell_0$ penalty for selecting $M$ over the data-generating $S$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:DeltaM}
\Delta_{MT}\;:=\;\sum_{j=1}^b \kappa_j(|M_j|-|T_j|). 
\end{equation}
and for any two models $Q,M\subseteq V$, we denote 
$$L_{QM}\;:=\;\|\bs X_{Q}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(Q)}\|^2-\|\bs X_{M}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2.$$
To lower bound $C(S)-C(M)$ we use that $L_{SM}$ can be expressed in terms of chi-squared variables.
For any over-fitted model $M \supset S$, we have $\Delta_{MS}>0$ and it is well-known that $-L_{SM} \sim \chi_{|M \setminus S|}^2$.
\david{I changed $L_{SM} \sim \chi_{|M \setminus S|}^2$ to $-L_{SM} \sim \chi_{|M \setminus S|}^2$}
Hence, if one sets large enough $\kappa_j$ (and thus $\Delta_{MS}$), then $NC(M)$ vanishes.
%vanishing of $\E\left( NC(M)\right)$ can be guaranteed by requiring the $\kappa_j$'s to be large enough.
For non-overfitted  models, the idea is to take the union model $Q_S=S\cup M$, and to note that $L_{SM}=L_{Q_SM}-L_{Q_SS}$. Since $M \subset Q_S$, we may use the next lemma to bound $L_{Q_SM}$ and hence also $L_{SM}$.
%Lemma \ref{lemma:s7} extends this results to non over-fitted models. The idea is to take the union model $Q=S\cup M$ and to note that $L_{SM}=L_{Q_SM}-L_{Q_SS}$.
%carefully the analyze its two components. 
%Consider $L_{SM}$, take $Q=S\cup M$ and write $L_{SM}=L_{Q_SM}-L_{Q_SS}$ and 
%$P_M=\bs X_{M}\left(\bs X_{M}^{\top} \bs X_{M}\right)^{-1} \bs X_{M}^{\top}$. 
%The next result gives the distribution of the components of $L_{SM}$. \paul{Maybe we should cite David's paper here to make it clear it is not new.} \david{yes}
\begin{lem}\label{lemma:s7}
    Let $M,Q$ be any two nested models such that $M \subseteq Q$. Then
$$
L_{QM}\;=\;\|\bs X_{Q}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(Q)}\|^2-\|\bs X_{M}\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2\;\sim\; \chi_{|Q\setminus M|}^2\left(\mu_{Q M}\right), 
$$
where $\chi_{k}^2(\mu)$ denotes the noncentral chi-squared distribution with $k$ degrees of freedom and noncentrality parameter $\mu$. The noncentrality parameter $\mu_{QM}$ is given by \begin{equation}\label{eq:muQM}
    \mu_{QM}\;\;:=\;\;\|\big(I_n-P_M\big) \bs X_{Q\setminus M} \bs \beta^{\star}_{Q\setminus M}\|^2
\end{equation}
where $P_M=\bs X_{M}\left(\bs X_{M}^{\top} \bs X_{M}\right)^{-1} \bs X_{M}^{\top}$.
\end{lem}

% Observe that the noncentrality parameter $\mu_{Q_SM}$ is the norm of the signal present in model $Q$ but missing with model $M$, projected in the space of residuals of model $M$.




% It convenient to rewrite the difference in norms of projections in \eqref{eq:boundexpNC} with respect to the norm for model $Q = M \cup S$:
% $$\|\bs X_S\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(S)}\|^2-\|\bs X_M\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2=\big(\|\bs X_S\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(S)}\|^2-\bs X_Q\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(Q)}\|^2\big)-\big(\|\bs X_M\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(M)}\|^2-\bs X_Q\tilde{\bs\beta}^{(Q)}\|^2\big)$$

%For an overfitting model $M$, $S\subset M$, $\Delta_{MS}$ positive, vanishing of $\E\left( NC(M)\right)$ can be guaranteed by requiring the $\kappa_j$'s to be large enough. Conversely, for $M$ such that $M\subset S$, $L_{SM}$ is positive and $\Delta_{MS}$ negative vanishing of $\E\left( NC(M)\right)$ can be guaranteed by requiring the $\kappa_j$'s to be small enough and that $L_{SM}$ is large enough. \david{How about cases where neither $S \subset M$ nor $M \subset S$?}

For example, by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}, for a non-overfitted \piotr{under-fitted?} $M\subset S$ we have $L_{SM}\sim \chi^2_{|M|-|S|}(\mu_{SM})$\piotr{negative degrees of freedom?}, which is centered around $|M|-|S|+\mu_{SM}$.
If the noncentrality parameter $\mu_{SM}$ is large enough, then $L_{SM}$ and $C(S)-C(M)$ are also large, which can be used to upper-bound $\mathbb{E}(NC(M))$ in Lemma \ref{lem:L0toL1convergence}.
%Then $L_{SM}$ taking large values in probability is guaranteed by sufficiently large noncentrality parameter $\mu_{Q_SM}$. 
Lemma \ref{lem:boundrho} shows that large $\mu_{Q_SM}$
%The following result shows the later 
can be achieved by setting betamin conditions on the signal strength and an eigenvalue condition on $\bs X$.  

\begin{lem}\label{lem:boundrho}
 For any $M\in\mathcal{M}$, let $Q_S=M \cup S$. The non-centrality parameter defined in \eqref{eq:muQM} satisfies: 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:musmbound}
    \mu_{Q_SM}\; \geq \;n\,\rho(\bs X)\,\sum_{j=1}^b|S_j\setminus M_j|\,{\beta_{\min,\,j}^{\star}}^2
\end{equation}

where \begin{equation}\label{eq:rhoX}
        \rho(\bs X)\,=\,\min_{M \in \mathcal{M}:\, S \not \subseteq M} \lambda_{\min}\big(\tfrac{1}{n} \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}^{\top}\left(I_n- P_M\right) \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}\big) \geq 0.
    \end{equation}
\end{lem}
\piotr{General math style comment. Avoid defining things in lemmas, propositions, etc. Here $\mu_{Q_SM}$, $P_M$, and $\rho$ are all defined in lemmas. Ideally results are just clean and short mathematical statements. (this is just a general guideline)}
The quantity $\rho(\bs X)$ is nonnegative and relates to 
%both the identifiability of $S$ and 
how distinguishable the other models $M\in \mathcal{M}$ are from $S$ \citep{infotheowainwright}. 
More specifically, $\tfrac{1}{n} \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}^{\top}\left(I_n- P_M\right) \boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}$ is the sample covariance matrix of the residuals resulting from regressing $\boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}$ on $\boldsymbol{X}_M$.
That is, $\rho(\bs X)$ measures how well one can approximate the 
$\boldsymbol{X}_{S \setminus M}$ with $\boldsymbol{X}_M$.
In an ideal orthonormal case where $\bs X^{\top}\bs X=n\I_p$, then $\rho(\bs X)=1$\piotr{Not $1/n$?}. 
%It is 0 if there exists a model $M\in\mathcal{M}$ such that $S \not\subseteq M$ and a column of $X_{S\setminus M}$ belongs to the column space of $X_M$. 
\david{I re-wrote the discussion of $\rho$ a little.}


\subsection{Selection based on block $\ell_0$ penalty}
\label{ssec:regression_mainresult}

We state two sufficient conditions for asymptotic support recovery. %with selector $\hat{S}^{b}$. 
Building on our previous discussion, the first requires the block penalties $\kappa_j$ to be large enough, %grow fast enough as we required the thresholds to grow in the Gaussian Sequence model. 
and the second is a betamin condition: 
\begin{enumerate}[start=6,label={(A\arabic*)}]
    \item For each block $j$, there exists $b_{j}\to \infty$ (as $n\to \infty$) such that for $n$ sufficiently large, %, and for all $j=1,\ldots,b$
    \david{Since we use $j=1,\ldots,b$, using $b_j$ may lead to confusion, maybe we can change for some other letter ($r_j$ or whatever)?}\piotr{agreed!}
\begin{equation}\label{cond:suffIreg}
    \kappa_j \;=\;\ln(p_j-s_j) + b_{j}
\end{equation}\item For each block $j$, there exists $c_{j}\to +\infty$ such that for $n$ sufficiently large, %for all $j=1,\ldots,b$
    \begin{equation}\label{cond:suffIIreg}
        \sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{8}}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j} \,- \,\sqrt{\kappa_j} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + c_{j} .
    \end{equation}
where $\gamma:=\frac12(1+\max_j \alpha_j)\in (\tfrac12,1)$, and
$\alpha_j:=\frac{\ln(p_j-s_j)}{\kappa_j}$.
\end{enumerate}
\paul{We could replace $(1-\gamma)$ by $\xi:=\min_j \tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\kappa_j-\ln(p_j-s_j)}{\kappa_j}$ or even $\xi:=\min_j \tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{b_j}{\kappa_j}$. It would make the condition more readable. Indeed $\min_j \tfrac{1}{2}\tfrac{\kappa_j-\ln(p_j-s_j)}{\kappa_j} = \tfrac{1}{2}(1-\max_j\tfrac{\ln(p_j-s_j)}{\kappa_j})$. It used to be like that but Piotr changed it think.}
\david{No strong feelings either way, but the current statement where it's clear that $\gamma \in (1/2,1)$ is bounded reads nicely to me.}\piotr{also no strong feelings}

These conditions are similar to A4-A5 formulated for the sequence model.
 By Proposition~\ref{prop:isthresh}, in the orthonormal %orthogonal and normalized 
 case $\bs X^{\top}\bs X=n\I_p$, setting block penalties $\kappa_j$ %$j=1, \ldots,b$ 
 is equivalent to hard thresholding with block thresholds $\tau_j=\sqrt{2 \kappa_j/n}$.
 Conditions A4-A5 can then be directly translated into conditions on $\kappa_j$, taking $\rho(\bs X)=1$. In that case, A6 is of the same order as A4, up to a term $b_j$ that can grow at an arbitrarily slow rate.
% Earlier requirements for selection consistency with block thresholding, A4 and A5, can then be directly translated into requirements on the block penalties assuming $\rho(\bs X)=1$. Upon comparison in that case, we observe that A6 is the same order as the earlier A4 up to a term $b_j$ that can grow at arbitrarily slow rate. 
The betamin assumption~A7 is the same as A5 up to, again, a term $c_j$ increasing at arbitrarily slow rate, and the factor $((1-\gamma)/2)^{1/2}$. This factor depends on how much larger the $\kappa_j$'s are than $\ln(p_j-s_j)$. It is smaller than 1 and bounded away from 0 unless $b_j= \kappa_j - \ln(p_j-s_j)= o(\ln(p_j-s_j))$ for some $j$.
\david{Earlier we said that $b_j$ can grow at an arbitrarily slow rate, but we now see that $b_j$ has to be at least of order $p_j-s_j$. How about saying: This factor depends on the choice of $b_j$ in A6. For example, by default we take $b_j=...$, in which case $\gamma$ is bounded below by ... Perhaps also relate the choice of $(b_j,c_j)$ to the oracle $\kappa_j^\star$'s discussed later in the theorem, like "an ideal choice of $(b_j,c_j)$ leads to oracle penalties $\kappa_j^\star$ given in Theorem 4.2 below" (and, if it helps, briefly explain how the oracle $(b_j,c_j)$ depend on $s_j,p_j,\beta_{\min}^\star$)} \paul{$b_j$ can grow at any rate.  For any growth rate you will discard asymptotically the overfitting models. When $b_j= o(\ln(p_j-s_j))$ for some $j$, the betamin requirement is stronger than $\Theta(\sqrt{\kappa_j} +\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + c_{j})$. To see it, the betamin requirement is essentially: $\sqrt{\frac{ n\rho(\bs X)}{8}}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}  \;=2(\max_j\frac{\ln(p_j-s_j)}{b_j}+1)(\sqrt{\kappa_j} +\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)}) + c_{j}$. It is not very intuitive to have such change in betamin condition i know. It is linked to a technicality of the proof. I have tried hard to get rid of it but could not. \\}
%As in the Gaussian sequence model, 
\david{The rest of the paragraph appeared after Theorem 4.1, but felt more natural here, as it discussed A6-A7. I'm not sure that it's strictly needed though, it basically repeats the notion that A6-A7 are very similar to the sequence model no?} In fact, from A6-A7 we can infer the smallest signals that can be recovered with $\hat{S}^{b}$. %as per conditions A6 and A7. 
We get the minimal betamin condition:
\begin{equation}\label{betamincond:minimalreg} \mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b \qquad
    \lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{8}}{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}{\sqrt{\ln(p_j-s_j)} + \sqrt{\ln(s_j)}} >1
\end{equation}
Note that, up to the factor $(1-\gamma)/8$, Expression \eqref{betamincond:minimalreg} is very similar to betamin conditions for the sequence model (Corollary \ref{cor:selconstimpossible}, Expression \eqref{eq:betamincon.suffthresh}).
\david{Before we mention a fctor $(1-\gamma)/2$, now it's $(1-\gamma)/2$?}


Finally, we remark that in our proof we discuss that one could take a betamin condition that is slightly less strict than A7, but we present A7 here to compare it more directly to A5 in the sequence model.
