We can now state our main theorem on the strong variable selection consistency of $\hat{S}^{b}$:
\begin{thm}\label{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}

Under assumptions A1, A2, A6 and, A7, we have
    % \begin{align}
    %  &\qquad \kappa_j > \ln(p_j-s_j)  \label{cond:suffIreg}\\
    %    &\qquad \sqrt{\frac{2(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{8}}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}  \geq \sqrt{\kappa_j}+{\sqrt{\ln(s_j)}}  .\label{cond:suffIIreg}
    % \end{align}
$$\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M}\setminus \{S\}}\E\left(NC(M)\right)\to 0 \qquad \mbox{and} \qquad P(\hat{S}^b = S) \to 1$$
\end{thm}

% \paul{A7 is not the tightest betamin condition I found, I had $\frac{2(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{16}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}^2 - \kappa_j = \ln(s_j) + \omega(1)$. But A7 matches more closely the betamin condition in the orthogonal case. It makes also subsequent results on oracle penalty and oracle rate much more aligned with orthogonal case, which simplifies the discussion of benefits of block $\ell_0$. It is a bit less honest though.}


Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} considers an additive penalty across blocks
$\sum_{j=1}^b \kappa_j|M_j|$. This additivity assumption can be easily relaxed in the proof. 
For example, the EBIC penalty is non-additive \citep{EBIC}.
In Appendix \ref{sec:appendixnonlinear} we give a consistency results for non-additive penalties, which in particular improves on known betamin conditions for the EBIC.
%The consequent result shown in Appendix \ref{sec:appendixnonlinear} includes selection with the EBIC \citep{EBIC} and improves on known betamin conditions for the criterion. 
It also shows that EBIC is consistent in a class of asymptotic regimes larger than that originally considered in \cite{EBIC}, and which includes ultra-high dimensions.

From the proof of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}, we can bound the convergence rate of $P(\hat S^{b}\neq S)$, given in Theorem \ref{prop:fullrecovreg}. We also give oracle block penalties that approximately optimize the bound, and provide the resulting oracle rate of convergence. 
In the statement, $\delta<1$ and $r_b>1$ should be understood as being arbitrarily close to 1 \paul{check 'arbitrarily close to 1'}.
\begin{thm}\label{prop:fullrecovreg}
Assume A6-A7. Then, for all sufficiently large $n$ and any $\delta \in (0,1)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rateconvreg}
 P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S)\;\leq\; 6(2^{2b}-2b)r_b\,\sum_{j=1}^b\,\, e^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big[\kappa_j-\ln(p_j-s_j)\big]} + 
    e^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big[(\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{8}}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j} - \sqrt{\kappa_j})^2 -\ln(s_j) \big]}
\end{equation}
where $r_b\to 1$ as $n\to +\infty$.

Moreover, suppose that the $\beta^\star_{\min,j}$ satisfy \eqref{betamincond:minimalreg} and the $\kappa_j$ are set at the oracle values:
	\begin{equation} \label{eq:oraclepenreg}
\sqrt{\kappa^\star_j\,} \;=\; \frac14\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{2}}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}+\sqrt{\frac{2}{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}}\frac{1}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}\big(\ln({p_j-s_j})-\ln({s_j})\big).
\end{equation}
Then for all sufficiently large $n$, A6 and A7 hold and for any $\delta \in (0,1)$: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:oraclerateconvreg}
    P(\hat{S}^{b} \neq S) \; \leq \; 12(2^{2b}-2b)r_b \sum_{j=1}^b e^{-\tfrac{\delta}{2}\big[\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{32}}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}^2-\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j),\ln(s_j)\}\big]},
\end{equation}
where $r_b\to 1$ as $n\to+\infty$.
\end{thm}

%\paul{This is not the tightest bound I found. I could also have $e^{-\delta\big[2(1-\gamma)\frac{n\rho(\bs X)}{16}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j}^2 - \kappa_j -\ln(s_j) \big]}$  instead of $e^{-\frac{\delta}{2}\big[(2(1-\gamma)\frac{n\rho(\bs X)}{16}{{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j} - \sqrt{\kappa_j})^2 -\ln(s_j) \big]}$. However the latter matches more closely the bound for the orthogonal case and makes $\sqrt{2\kappa^*/n} = \tau^*$ up to a $\frac{\sqrt{2(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}}{4}$ factor.\\

% \end{equation*}}
\subsection{Necessary conditions for selection consistency}

We derive conditions on the $\kappa_j$ and $\beta_{min,j}$ that are necessary for consistent variable selection. The necessary condition on $\kappa_j$ follow directly from controlling the pseudo posterior probabilities of over-fitted models. Those on $\beta_{min,j}$ follow from studying the convergence of the pseudo-posterior probability for $S$ under no assumption on minimum signal strength. 

% That study also leads to a result key to the derivation of the selection properties of the data analysis methods in Section~\ref{sec:informed_l0}.

The vanishing of pseudo-posterior probabilities of over-fitted models is driven by the strength of the $\kappa_j$. Variable selection consistency requires the $\kappa_j$ to be large enough for the pseudo-posterior probabilities of all the over-fitted models to be small, in particular the least penalized one. For every $j=1,\ldots,b$, denote the set of models that over-fit by only one variable from block $B_j$:
$$O_j \:=\,\{M\in \mathcal{M}\;|\; M=S\cup\{l\} \text{ where } l\in B_j\setminus S_j\}$$
For every $j=1,\ldots,b$, the recovery of $S$ implies that $\max_{M\in O_j}NC(M)/NC(S)<1$ with probability goign to 1 as $n$ grows. For every $M\in O_j$, the ratio $NC(M)/NC(S)$ grows with $L_{MS}$ which, by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}, is $\chi^2_1$ distributed. There exists then, for every $M\in O_j$, $Z_M \sim N(0,1)$ such that $L_{MS}=Z_M^2$. A necessary condition on $\kappa_j$ follows from the properties of the maximum of the correlated $Z_M$. Denote the smallest eigenvalue of their correlation matrix:
\begin{equation}
    \underline{\lambda}_j\;:=\;\lambda_{min}(R^j)\;\;\text{where} \;\;\forall m,n\;\;R^j_{m,n}=\operatorname{corr}\big(Z_M,Z_N\big),\;\;M,N \in O_j
\end{equation}
\paul{note it is not the correlation matrix of the $\sqrt{L_{MS}}=|Z_M|$. This result is based on \cite{hartigan} that gives a lower bound for maximum of correlated Gaussians and not correlated \textbf{folded} Gaussians. The lower bound is probably the same but proving it would require additional lemmas.}
\paul{the notation for the correlation matrix is a bit sloppy but I think rather clear.}
A lower bound on the maximum of the $Z_M$ gives the result.
\begin{prop}\label{prop:nectypeIreg}
     If for any $j=1,\ldots,b$,  $\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\kappa_j}{\underline{\lambda}_j^2\ln(p_j-s_j)} <1$, then under assumptions A1 and A2, $P(\hat{S}^b=S) \to 0$.
\end{prop}

\paul{Note in passing: \cite{hartigan} nicely complements the result Piotr found on independence being the "worst case" for the maximum of Gaussians.\\
Interestingly it suggests that we could set lower penalty when inactive variables are highly correlated between each other. I remember very well that someone in Toronto remarked that it was somewhat strange that sufficient conditions for penalty strength did not depend on the level of correlation between variables. I could not answer well back then!}

Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIreg} shows that 
a necessary condition to recover S asymptotically, %and model recovery 
is: 
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIallblockpen}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\kappa_j}{\underline{\lambda}_j^2\ln(p_j-s_j)}\;\geq\;1\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.
\end{equation}
When the inactive variables are orthogonal, that is $\bs X_{V\setminus S}^{\top}\bs X_{V\setminus S}=nI$, for every $j=1,\ldots,b$ and $M\in O_j$, the $Z_M$ are independent and $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$. Under orthogonal design, condition~\eqref{cond:necIallblockpen} then recovers condition~\eqref{cond:necIallblockthresh}.

Consider now an under-fitted model $M\subset S$. The recovery of $S$ implies that with probability going to 1, $NC(M)/NC(S)<1$. By definition of $NC$ the ratio grows with penalties $\kappa_j$ and decreases with $L_{SM}$, distributed $\chi^2_{|S\setminus M|}(\mu_{SM})$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}. Small values of the ratio are  thenattained only if $\mu_{SM}$ grows fast enough compared to the $\kappa_j$. Lemma~\ref{lem:upperboundnoncentral} shows that the growth $\mu_{SM}$ is bounded by the growth of the largest active signals in $S$ that is not in $M$.
\begin{lem}\label{lem:upperboundnoncentral}
    For any $T\subseteq S$, $$\mu_{ST} \leq n\,\bar{\lambda}\,\sum_{j=1}^b|S_j\setminus T_j|\,\max_{i\in S_j\setminus T_j}{\beta_{i}^{\star}}^2 \quad \text{where}\quad \bar{\lambda}\,:=\,\lambda_{\max}\Big(\frac1n \bs X_{S}^\top\bs X_{S}\Big)$$
\end{lem}

For a choice of penalties $\kappa_j$ for $j=1,\ldots,b$, we denote the set of active signals that are small with respect to those penalties: 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:defss}
    S_j^{S}\;:=\;\Bigg\{\beta_i^{\star} \in S_j \,\Big|\,\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}|\beta_{i}^{\star}|=o\big(\sqrt{\kappa_j}\big) \Bigg\}
\end{equation}
When $S^S=\cup_{j=1}^b S_j^S \neq \emptyset$ and taking $M=S\setminus S^S$, the missing active signals in $M$ are all small compared to the $\kappa_j$. By the properties of the $\chi^2$ distribution, we then establish the following convergence of the probabilities that $NC(M)/NC(S)<1$ and of recovery of $S$.

% The vanishing of pseudo-posterior probabilities of any  is driven by the strength of the missing active signals. Selection consistency requires all active signal to be large enough including the smallest ones. We distinguish, within each block $B_j$ of $S$, small active signals from the rest of active signals. 

% The subset $S_j^{S}$ contains the signals that are small with respect to the block penalty $\kappa_j$. The quantity $\bar{\lambda}$ is the largest eigenvalue of the Schur complement of $\bs X_{S^S}$ on $\bs X_S$ and is bounded away from 0 for $\bs X_S$ full rank. \paul{Bounded away from 0 by interlacing. I can't say much more about this quantity for nomw, it just appears when bounding above a non-centrality parameter as $\rho(X)$ appeared in bounding below noncentrality parameters. It's probably related to how the columns in $X_{S^S}$ are correlated to the rest of $X_S$.}
% \paul{The distinction between $S^I$ and $S^L$ is not strictly necessary here but will be useful for a result in the data analysis section.}

% If $S^S\neq\emptyset$, the recovery of $S$ implies that $NC(S\setminus S^S)/NC(S)<1$ with probability going to 1 as $n$ grows. By definition of $NC$ the ratio grows with penalties $\kappa_j$ and shrinks with $L_{S(S\setminus S^S)}$ distributed $\chi^2_{|S^S|}(\mu_{S(S\setminus S^S)})$ by Lemma~\ref{lemma:s7}. 

% Selection Observe that when $S^S\neq\emptyset$, $S\neq S^L\cup S^I$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:L0toL1convergence} (ii) the convergence of $NC(S)$ can be obtained by studying a function of the pairwise comparison of $C(S)$ and $C(S^L\cup S^I)$, which leads to the following result. 

\begin{prop}\label{prop:nectypeIIreg}
    If $S^S\neq\emptyset$ and for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, $\kappa_j\to \infty$, then, under assumptions A1,
    $$P\Big(\frac{NC(S\setminus S^S)}{NC(S)}<1\Big) \to 0 \quad\text{and}\quad P(\hat{S}^b=S)\to 0$$
\end{prop}
Property~\ref{prop:nectypeIIreg} shows that for any increasing penalty, when $S$ contains signals that are too small with respect to that penalty, the smaller model $S\setminus S^S$ is favored at the expense of $S$ and recovery fails. It follows that a necessary condition for variable selection consistency is that for all $j=1,\ldots,b$:
\begin{equation}\label{cond:necIIallblockpen}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}}{\sqrt{\kappa_j}}\;>\;0\qquad\mbox{for all }j=1,\ldots,b.
\end{equation}
When active variables are orthogonal, $\bs X_S^{\top}\bs X_S = nI$, $\bar{\lambda}=1$. Under orthogonal design, the general condition~\eqref{cond:necIIallblockpen} is milder and less tight than the previously derived condition~\eqref{cond:necIallblockthresh}.

A corollary of Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIreg} and Proposition~\ref{prop:nectypeIIreg} is as follows. 
\begin{cor}\label{cor:necrecovreg}
    If for some $j\in\{1,\ldots,b\}$   $\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}}{\sqrt{\underline{\lambda}_j\ln(p_j-s_j)}} = 0$, then, under assumption A1 and A2,  $P(\hat{S}^b=S)\not\to 1$
\end{cor}
Corollary~\ref{cor:necrecovreg} yields a necessary condition for consistent variables selection assuming A1 and A2:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betaminnecreg}
\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{n\bar{\lambda}}\beta_{\min,j}^{\star}}{\sqrt{\underline{\lambda}_j\ln(p_j-s_j)}} > 0
\end{equation}
Condition \eqref{eq:betaminnecreg} is a general necessary betamin condition that applies in correlated, orthogonal, low and high dimensional setting, for $s$ fixed or diverging. In an orthogonal design such that $\bs X^{\top} \bs X=nI$, for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, $\underline{\lambda}_j=1$ and $\bar{\lambda}=1$. Previously derived necessary condition \eqref{eq:betamin} for the orthogonal case with $s$ diverging is then stricter and tighter that Condition \eqref{eq:betaminnecreg} in that particular setting.


\subsection{Gains and losses of block penalties}
%\subsection{Gains and losses of penalizing base on externally defined blocks}
\label{ssec:regression_gains}

%\paul{added this outline }
%We now discuss the benefits of penalizing based on externally defined blocks. 
We discuss separately the benefits in the conditions for variable selection consistency and those in the convergence rate. The main message %takeaway 
is that benefits discussed in the sequence model in Section~\ref{sec:properties_orth} extend to linear regression.
\bigskip

\david{Throughout, the notion of minimal betamin condition wasn't clear to me. Probably just me being obtuse, but this is a non-standard notion that could confuse readers. People are used to hearing about betamin conditions, and about whether they are either necessary or sufficient. Would it be possible to frame them using this standard terminology? For example, the discussion below would be clearer if one said that "these are necessary betamin conditions for standard and for block penalties", or something along those lines.}

\noindent Assume without loss of generality that $\beta_{\min}^{\star}$ is located in block $b$.  By \eqref{betamincond:minimalreg}, the minimal betamin conditions for the block informed $\ell_0$ penalty $\hat{S}^b$ and standard $\ell_0$ penalty $\hat{S}^1$ are respectively: 
$$\lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\sqrt{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)/8}\,{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}}{\sqrt{\ln(p_b-s_b)} + \sqrt{\ln(s_b)}} >1 \qquad \mbox{and} \qquad \lim_{n\to \infty}\frac{\sqrt{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)/8}\,{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}}{\sqrt{\ln(p-s)} + \sqrt{\ln(s)}} >1$$
The requirements on $\beta_{\min,j}^\star$ and $\beta_{\min}^\star$ again relate to
%The relevant quantities to compare the minimal requirement on the growth rate of betamin are thus again 
$\sqrt{\ln(p_b-s_b)} + \sqrt{\ln(s_b)}$ and $\sqrt{\ln(p-s)} + \sqrt{\ln(s)}$, hence the discussion in Section \ref{sec:prosconsblock} on the benefits in betamin conditions extend directly to linear regression.
%The discussion in Section \ref{sec:prosconsblock} on the benefits in terms of betamin conditions of the block informed $\ell_0$ penalty in the %Gaussian 
%sequence model then extend to linear regression. %the linear regression settings. 
In particular, there are linear regression settings where variable selection consistency is not possible with $\hat{S}^1$, but is with $\hat{S}^b$. For example, in a simplest case where $\bs X^\top \bs X=n I_p$, consistency results for the sequence model apply here
%When one assumes $\bs X$ satisfies $\bs X^\top \bs X=n I_p$, results on selection in the Gaussian sequence model apply 
and Corollary \ref{cor:selconstimpossible} outlines such instances.

Let ${OR}^b_{reg}$ be the oracle convergence rate for $\hat{S}^{b}$ in Theorem~\ref{prop:fullrecovreg}, and ${OR}_{reg}$ that for $\hat{S}^{1}$ . Then
\begin{equation*}
    \frac{{OR}^b_{reg}}{{OR}_{reg}}=(2^{2b-1}-b)\frac{r_b}{r_1}\sum_{j=1}^be^{-\frac{\delta}{2}\left[\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{32}\left({\beta^\star_{\min,j}}^2-{\beta_{\min}^\star}^2\right)+\max\{\ln(p-s),\ln(s)\}-\max\{\ln(p_j-s_j),\ln(s_j)\}\right]}
\end{equation*}
which is essentially the same as in the sequence model %the ratio in the analysis of the Gaussian Sequence model 
in \eqref{eq:ratioCR}, up to certain %mainly constant 
factors. 
Specifically, $r_b/r_1$ and $\delta$ are both close to 1, whereas $(1-\gamma) \rho(\bs X)/32$ slows the convergence rate gains %gain in convergence rate with $\hat{S}^{b}$ 
but does not alter the essence of the ratio %monotonicity and asymptotic behaviour of the ratio, 
as long as $\rho(\bs X)$ remains bounded above 0. The factor $2^{2b-1}-b$ highlights a potential limitation: guarantees of gains with block $\ell_0$ penalties deteriorate when one considers a large number of blocks $b$.
We remark that such deterioration may be a consequence of our proof strategy, rather than an inherent limitation of block penalties. Studying cases with large $b$, including $b \to \infty$, is left as future research.
%and may vanish when $b\to \infty$.

\section{Data analysis with informed $\ell_0$ penalization}\label{sec:informed_l0}

\david{I made a number of edits to the section, to clarify the connection between block $\ell_0$ and the Bayesian paradigm, particularly for non-Bayesian readers. Piotr in particular please chime in, if things don't make sense.}

In Sections~\ref{sec:properties_orth} and~\ref{sec:properties_legression}, we derived oracle properties for block penalties where one sets the penalties to oracle values.
We now %In this section we 
propose two data analysis methods anchored in a Bayesian perspective %on the problem to incorporate external information in selection. %and realize benefits discussed in sections ~\ref{sec:properties_orth} and ~\ref{sec:properties_legression}.
that do not require oracle values, e.g. that adapt to the unknown sparsity in the data-generating truth.
A Bayesian approach provides a natural estimator of the number of truly active variables in each block and thus, by using empirical Bayes, a straightforward way to adapt penalties to sparsity in each block. % closer to the oracle \eqref{eq:oraclepenreg}.
\\
\\
Consider a Bayesian framework for \eqref{eq:linearmodel} where %$M$ is taken as a random vector over the space of models. More precisely let 
the model $M$ is a $p$-dimensional vector of variable inclusion indicators $m_i=I(\beta_i \neq 0)$. Consider a joint prior on parameters and models 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bayesianframework}
    p(\boldsymbol{\beta},M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})=p( \boldsymbol{\beta} \mid  M) p(M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})
\end{equation}
where $p(M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})$ is the prior probability of model $M$, which depends on hyperparameter $\bs \theta$,
%distribution over  the model space with hyperparameter $\bs \theta$ 
and $p( \boldsymbol{\beta} \mid  M)$ is a prior on regression coefficients given the model. %(e.g Zellner's g-prior \cite{zellner}). % or product moment prior \cite{pmom})
\david{I removed the reference to non-local priors because for these you don't get the usual asymptotic equivalence with $\ell_0$ penalties. I also explained how the Bayesian framework is linked to block penalties. 
%I think that we can also remove the sentence to Zellner's prior, because in the end we just use the BIC approx.
}

Posterior model probabilities are $p(M \mid \bs y, \bs \theta) \propto p(\bs y \mid M) p(M \mid \bs \theta)$, where $p(\bs y \mid M)$ is the so-called marginal likelihood of model $M$. The BIC approximation \citep{BIC} gives
\begin{align*}
 p( \bs y  \mid M)\approx p(\bs y \mid \hat{\bs \beta}_M)n^{-|M|/2}.
\end{align*}
\paul{It was $p( M \mid \bs y ) \approx$, I haved changed to $p( \bs y  \mid M) \approx$. That's correct no?}
It follows that the posterior model probabilities satisfy
\begin{align*}
\ln p(M \mid\bs y) \approx \ln p(\bs y \mid \hat{\bs \beta}_M) - \frac{1}{2}\ln(n)|M| + \ln p(M\mid\bs \theta) + \text{constant}
\end{align*}
\paul{I have added equation above so it is more explicit to a reader non familiar with l0 /Bayesian selection.}
and any block or standard $\ell_0$ penalty selector discussed in earlier sections can be written as $\hat{M}= \arg\max_M p(M \mid \bs y)$. 
In particular, suppose that variable inclusions are independent a priori, and that inclusion probabilities are constant within each block. That is,
\begin{align} \label{eq:block_modelprior}
p( M \mid \boldsymbol{\theta})=
        \prod_{i=1}^p \operatorname{Bern}\left(m_i ; \theta_i\right) I(M \in \mathcal{M}) \mbox{, where } \forall \, i\in B_j, \: \theta_i=\theta^{(j)}
\end{align}
then $\ln p(M \mid \bs \theta)$ defines the additive block penalties studied in Section \ref{sec:properties_legression} for block penalties:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:blockpenpriorinclu}
    \kappa_j=\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)+\ln\big({\theta^{(j)}}^{-1}-1\big).
\end{equation}

\paul{I think it is better to define $\hat{s}_j$ from normalized scores directly. Because we see it as a Bayesian object only for the connection to Empirical Bayes. If we keep it as it was: defining it as sum of posterior inclusion prop with a paragraph explaining that we the same notation for a sum of normalized criterion values, that paragraph would not very visible so reader could get lost. I am suggesting the following writeup}

This connection motivates an estimator of the number of truly active variables $s_j$ for any block $\ell_0$ procedure:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shat}
    \widehat{s}_j \,:=\, \sum_{i\in B_j}\,\,\sum_{M\in \mathcal{M} | i \in M}NC(M)
\end{equation}
The normalized score $NC(M)$ can be seen as the posterior probability for model $M$ in Bayesian framework \eqref{eq:bayesianframework}. Replacing normalized scores  by posterior model probabilities in \eqref{eq:shat} gives:
\begin{equation*}
    \sum_{i\in B_j}\,\,\sum_{M\in \mathcal{M} | i \in M}P(M\mid \bs y) = \sum_{i\in B_j}P(\beta_i \neq 0 \mid \bs y) = E\Big(\frac{s_j}{p_j} \mid \bs y\Big)
\end{equation*}
From a Bayesian perspective, $\widehat{s}_j$ is then the posterior mean of $s_j$. The estimator of the proportion of truly active variables $\widehat{s}_j/p_j$ can also be formally justified as an empirical Bayes procedure. The empirical Bayes estimator of $\bs \theta$ maximizes the marginal likelihood of $\bs y$ given $\bs \theta$ and is defined as:
\begin{align}
\hat{\bs \theta}^{EB}\,:=\, \arg\max_{\bs \theta} p(\bs y \mid \bs \theta)=
\arg\max_{\bs \theta} \int p(\bs y \mid \bs \beta, M) d P(\bs \beta, M \mid \bs \theta).
\nonumber
\end{align}
 Lemma \ref{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret} below shows that in the Bayesian framework \eqref{eq:block_modelprior}, $\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$ corresponds to the empirical Bayes estimate of $\bs \theta$.
\begin{lem}\label{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret} For $p(M \mid \bs \theta)$ in \eqref{eq:block_modelprior}, the empirical Bayes estimator satisfies $$\hat{\theta}^{EB}_i = \frac{1}{p_j}\sum_{i\in B_j}P(\beta_i \neq 0 \mid \bs y)$$
\end{lem}

From the definition in \eqref{eq:shat}, simple algebra gives the following bounds  on the bias of $\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$.
% \begin{lem}\label{lem:shatconsistence}
%  For $\hat{S}^b$ as in \eqref{eq:Mhat} :
% $$\Big|\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}-\frac{s_j}{p_j}\Big|\leq \sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | M_i \neq S_i}NC(M)$$
% \end{lem}
\begin{lem}\label{lem:shatconsistence}
 Estimator $\widehat{s}_j$ in \eqref{eq:shat} satisfies:$$- \frac{s_j}{p_j}\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | |M_j| < s_j}\E(NC(M)) \,\, \leq \E\Big(\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}\Big)-\frac{s_j}{p_j}\leq \,\,\frac{p_j-s_j}{p_j}\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | |M_j| \geq s_j}\E(NC(M))$$

\end{lem}

$$- \sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | |M_j| < s_j}\frac{s_j-|M_j|}{p_j}\E(NC(M)) \,\, \leq \E(\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j})-\frac{s_j}{p_j}\leq \,\,\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | |M_j| \geq s_j}\frac{|M_j|-s_j}{p_j}\E(NC(M))$$
Sufficient conditions for consistency of $\hat{s}_j/p_j$ are slightly weaker than selection but not so much.
I am not detailing them because we don't need selection consistency of $\hat{s}_j/p_j$ for the consistency of the data analysis methods.\\
Also the inequalities above hold almost surely, not only in expectation.\\}
Lemma~\ref{lem:shatconsistence} shows that the upward bias of $\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$ is bounded above by the expected sum of scores of models larger than the true model in block $B_j$. Conversely, downward bias is bounded by the expected scores of smaller models. A immediate corollary of the Lemma is that $\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$ is a consistent estimator of the proportion of true active variables if the block $\ell_0$ procedure is strongly variable selection consistent. Although not detailed here, consistency of $\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$ can be shown to hold under milder conditions.

We derive next bounds on the bias of $\widehat{s}_j$ when the sufficient conditions on signal strength are not met. To do so, we study the convergence of the sum of pseudo-posterior probabilities under no assumption on the minimal signal strength. In particular, we show the sum of posterior probabilities of all but a restricted set  of models vanishes in expectation for sufficiently large penalties. 

In \eqref{eq:defss}, we defined, for a choice of penalties $\kappa_j$, the set of signals that small with respect to those penalties. We now distinguish between large and intermediate active signals in $S \setminus S^S$. For any $j=1,\ldots,b$, $\gamma$ and $c_j$ defined as in A7, let: 
\begin{align}\label{eq:defslsi}
 \begin{split}
    &S_j^{L}\;:=\;\Bigg\{\beta_i^{\star} \in S_j \,\Big|\,\sqrt{\frac{(1-\gamma) n\rho(\bs X)}{8}}{\beta_{i}^{\star}} \,- \,\sqrt{\kappa_j} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + c_{j}\Bigg\}\\
    &S_j^{I}\;:=\;S_j \setminus \big(S_j^L \cup S_j^S\big)
 \end{split}
\end{align}

The subset $S_j^{L}$ gathers the large signals in $S_j$ in the sense that they satisfy assumption~A7. The subset $S_j^{I}$ gathers the signals in $S_j$ that are neither large nor small. Consider the set of models that contain $S^L=\cup_{j=1}^b S^L_j$ and some or none of the signals in $S^I=\cup_{j=1}^b S^I_j$.
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{T} \;:=\;\Big\{M \in \mathcal{M} \,\big|\, M\,=\, S^L\,\cup\,R,\; R\in \mathcal{P}\big(S^I\big)  \Big\}
\end{equation}
\paul{we need to say $ \mathcal{P}()$ is the power set}
We follow a proof strategy essentially similar to that Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} to show, assuming only sufficiently large penalties, the vanishing the expected sum of pseudo-probabilities of all models but the ones in $\mathcal{T}$. The main difference with respect to the proof of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} is that, for any $M\not\in \mathcal{T}$, we bound $\E(NC(M))$ by a function of $C(M)-C(T)$ for a well chosen model $T$ in $\mathcal{T}$. 
\begin{thm}\label{thm:convtoT}
    Under assumptions A1, A2 and A6, $\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M}\setminus \{\mathcal{T}\}} \E\left(NC(M)\right)\to 0$
\end{thm}
\paul{I am assuming the $\beta_{\min}$ remains bounded away from 0 in this theorem for a technical reason that I am too lazy to overcome. Should I say it somewhere}

\paul{I could change the condition $|S^I|=O(1)$ to $\forall j$ $|S^I|=o(b_j)$ and $|S^I|=o(c_j)$ }
Theorem~\ref{thm:convtoT} shows that, asymptotically, with only assuming sufficiently large penalties, all small active and inactive signals are discarded but all large signals in $S^L$ are retained. Those in $S^I$ may or may not be retained. A first consequence, gathered in Lemma~\ref{lem:convtoTprob} in Auxiliary Results \ref{auxiliary}, is that $\hat{S}^b\in \mathcal{T}$ with probability going to 1 \paul{it is not auxiliary to anything. It is an interesting result but I don't know where to put it.}. Theorem~\ref{thm:convtoT} also allows to establish the following bounds on the bias of $\hat{s}_j$.

\end{prop}

The two selection methods that we propose rely on %the estimator 
$\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$ to adapt the $\ell_0$ penalty in each block. 
%They are motivated by the well-studied equivalence, under mild regularity conditions, between the mode of the posterior distribution $p(M \mid\bs y,\bs\theta)$ and the solution of the $\ell_0$ selection problem \citep{BIC,EBIC} \paul{can we just cite similar results (I cite the BIC paper) or do we have to write a lemma?} \david{I think it's ok to just reference the BIC and EBIC papers}.

%For the prior in Lemma~\ref{lem:shatconsistence}, under mild regularity conditions, we get:
%\begin{equation}\label{eq:equivl0bayes}

%\end{equation}
The first method builds on the connection in \eqref{eq:blockpenpriorinclu} and follows the empirical Bayes approach of Lemma~\ref{lem:empiricalbayesinterpret} to set block penalties.\\ \\
\david{On the discussion surrounding Method 1. (a) Saying that we run a BVS method sounds a bit vague. A natural strategy would be to use EBIC pseudo-posterior probabilities in Step 1, as a reasonable default that's coherent with our using EBIC to approximate posterior model probabilities. We can add a mention that one could alternatively consider other BVS methods, if so desired. But then, in Step 2 the coherent thing would be to take $\hat{M}= \arg\max p(M \mid \bs y)$, using the same priors that in Step 1, except that one uses $\hat{\theta}_i^{EB}$. That is, we either use $\ell_0$ in both steps, or a Bayesian framework in both steps. (b) To motivate Method 2, we say that Method 1 requires variable selection consistency and $s=o(n)$. It's not clear how Method 2 avoids this, e.g. in the discussion after Method 2 we say that it asymptotically uses $\kappa_j= \ln(p_j - s_j) + 0.5 \ln(n)$, and that this requires variable selection consistency in Step 1. %The first method we propose follows from setting the $\theta^{(j)}$'s in \eqref{eq:equivl0bayes} to an estimate of their values under the empirical Bayes approach in Lemma~\ref{lem:shatconsistence}b). 
%\paul{alternative phrasing I thought of: The first method we propose approximates the empirical Bayes in Lemma~\ref{lem:shatconsistence}b) by setting $\theta^{(j)}$ to values approaching their empirical Bayes estimates}\\ \\
\textbf{Method 1 - Empirical Bayes:}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Obtain, for every $M\in \mathcal{M}$ and $\ell_0$ penalty $\kappa_j=\kappa^{\circ}=\ln(p)+\frac{1}{2}\ln(n)$ for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, the normalized score $NC^{\circ}(M)$  and compute $\frac{\hat{s}_j}{p_j}$ for every $j=1,\ldots,b$.
    \item Select the model:
    \begin{equation*}
    \hat{S}^{EB}:= \arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j| \right\}
    \text{where} \,\,\forall j\,\,\kappa_j = \ln\bigg(\frac{p_j}{\hat{s}_j}-1\bigg)+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)
    \end{equation*}
    % \begin{gather*}
    % \hat{S}^{EB}:= \arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j| \right\}\\
    % \text{where} \,\,\kappa_j = \ln\bigg(\frac{p_j}{\min\{\hat{s}_j,\ln(n^{\frac{1}{4}})\}}{}-1\bigg)+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)\,\,\text{for all } j=1,\ldots,b
    % \end{gather*}
\end{enumerate}
Step 1 approximates posterior model probabilities under uniform prior inclusion probabilities $(p+1)^{-1}$ and estimates of the proportion of truly active coefficients in every block. Step 2 selects a model with block penalties approximating the empirical Bayes choice for prior inclusion probabilities in the equivalent Bayesian framework \eqref{eq:block_modelprior}. 

For the selection consistency of $\hat{S}^{EB}$ to be guaranteed by Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}, step 2 penalties need to meet assumption~A6 and $\frac{\widehat{s}_j}{p_j}$ should not be widely overestimated. By Lemma~\ref{lem:shatconsistence}, this can be achieved by controlling the scores of large models. On the contrary, underestimation stemming from the scores of small models leads to larger step 2 penalties and stricter assumptions for the signals to meet assumption~A7. Consider the following assumptions:
\begin{enumerate}[start=8,label={(A\arabic*)}]
    \item $n \, p^{-l} \to \infty$ for some $l>0$, $\sqrt{n}s^{-1} \to +\infty$ and $\frac{s}{s_j}<\infty$ for all $j=1,\ldots,b$\\
\item For each block $j$, there exists $c_{j}\to +\infty$ such that for $n$ sufficiently large, %for all $j=1,\ldots,b$
    \begin{equation*}
        (\gamma n\rho(\bs X))^{\frac{1}{2}}{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j \,- \,\sqrt{\ln\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{n}(p_j-s_j)}{s_j}\bigg)+\ln\bigg(\frac{1+\frac{s_j}{p_j-s_j}\pi_j}{1-\pi_j}\bigg)} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + c_{j} .
    \end{equation*}
where $\gamma:=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[1+2l^{-1}+2\ln^{-1}(n)\max_j\ln\bigg(\frac{1+\frac{s_j}{p_j-s_j}\pi_j}{1-\pi_j}\bigg)\bigg]^{-1}$ and \\
$\pi_j:=\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | |M_j| < s_j}\frac{s_j-|M_j|}{s_j}NC^{\circ}(M)$.
\end{enumerate}

Assumption A8 guarantees that the choice of step 1 penalty $\kappa^{\circ}$ is strong enough for the scores of large models to vanish quickly and the step 2 penalties to meet assumption~A6. Assumption A9 requires a a sparse setting where $s=o(\sqrt{n})$. Its other requirement $sp^{-l}\to \infty$ for some $l>0$ is rather mild as it allows any regime where $p=n^k$ for some $k>0$. It excludes nonetheless ultra high dimension where $p=e^{a\,n}$ for some $a>0$. The quantity $\pi_j$ is tightly connected to the probability of type II error in step 1. Bounds on $\pi_j$ can be obtained by requiring a betamin assumption, but realistic and relevant approximations depend on the values of all active coefficients. By Lemma XXX in XXX, when $\max_{i\in S_j}|\beta^*_i| > \kappa^{\circ}$, $\pi_j$ remains bounded away from $1$, $\gamma$ can then be simplified to $\frac{1}{32}[1+l^{-1}]^{-1}$ and whenever $s_j(p_j-s_j)^{-1}<\infty$, the quantity $\ln\big((1+\frac{s_j}{p_j-s_j}\pi_j)(1-\pi_j)^{-1}\big)$ is guaranteed to be finite . 

% Selector $\hat{S}^{EB}$ asymptotically recovers $\hat{S}^{b}$ with $\kappa_j = \ln\big(\tfrac{p_j}{s_j}-1\big) + \min\{\tfrac{1}{4}\ln(n)\}$ 
\begin{thm}\label{theo:empbayesselconsist}
    Under assumptions A1, A2, A8 and, A9, 
$$P(\hat{S}^{EB} = S) \to 1$$
\end{thm}


The empirical Bayes method (method 1) requires restrictive assumptions on the total and within block numbers of active coefficients to meet the conditions of Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel}. We introduce a second method that can be shown to be selection consistent with Theorem~\ref{theo:suffcondlinearmodel} in a wider class of regimes including ultra-high dimension.\\ \\
\textbf{Method 2: Informed block $\ell_0$ criterion}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Obtain, for every $M\in \mathcal{M}$ and $\ell_0$ penalty $\kappa_j=\kappa^{\circ}=\ln(p)+\frac{1}{2}\ln(n)$ for all $j=1,\ldots,b$, the normalized score $NC^{\circ}(M)$  and compute $\frac{\hat{s}_j}{p_j}$ for every $j=1,\ldots,b$.
    \item Select the model:
    \begin{equation*}
    \hat{S}^{IB\ell_0}:= \arg\max_{M\in \mathcal{M}} \left\{\max_{\beta\in \mathcal{L}_M}\ell(\bs y; \bs \beta)-\sum_{j=1}^b\kappa_j |M_j| \right\}
    \text{where} \,\,\forall j\,\,\kappa_j = \ln(p_j-\hat{s}_j)+ \tfrac{1}{2}\ln(n)
    \end{equation*}  
\end{enumerate}

Step 1 of the informed block $\ell_0$ criterion method is the same as in the empirical Bayes method. It approximates posterior model probabilities under uniform prior inclusion probabilities $(p+1)^{-1}$ and estimates the proportion of active coefficients in every block. Step 2 selects a model with block penalties set at larger values than in the empirical Bayes method. Those block penalties thus more easily meet condition $A6$. Consider the following assumption:
\begin{enumerate}[start=10,label={(A\arabic*)}]
\item For each block $j$, there exists $c_{j}\to +\infty$ such that for $n$ sufficiently large, %for all $j=1,\ldots,b$
    \begin{equation*}
        \sqrt{\gamma n\rho(\bs X)}{\beta_{\min}^{\star}}_j \,- \,\sqrt{\ln(\sqrt{n}(p_j-s_j))+\ln\Big(1+\frac{s_j}{p_j-s_j}\pi_j\Big)} \;=\; \sqrt{\ln(s_j)} + c_{j} .
    \end{equation*}
where $\gamma:=\frac{1}{16}\bigg[\frac{\ln(\sqrt{n})}{\ln(p)+\ln(\sqrt{n})}\bigg]$ and 
$\pi_j:=\sum_{M \in \mathcal{M} | |M_j| < s_j}\frac{s_j-|M_j|}{s_j}NC^{\circ}(M)$.
\end{enumerate}
Under assumption A1 and A2, any choice of  increasing penalty $\kappa^{\circ}$ is sufficient for the step 2 penalties in method 2 to meet assumption~A6. It is in particular the case for the choice $\kappa^{\circ}$. Assumption A10 is a betamin condition that is required to ensure the signals are large enough to meet assumption~A7. The betamin assumption~A10 for method 2 is stricter than the betamin assumption~A9 for method 1.

\begin{thm}
    Under assumptions A1, A2, A10, 
$$P(\hat{S}^{IB\ell_0} = S) \to 1$$
\end{thm}

\section{Empirical results}\label{sec:simulation}

\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}

We provided an understanding of how external information can ease variable selection in the Gaussian Sequence model and high-dimensional linear regression. We studied the case where external information helps partition variables into blocks with potentially distinct characteristics and introduced externally-informed $\ell_0$ selectors that let the penalty depend on those blocks. We showed that an oracle externally-informed selector converges faster and has less strict betamin condition than the standard $\ell_0$ oracle. We also provided concrete data analysis methods that incorporate external information to improve variable selection properties without requiring oracle knowledge.

A question that immediately arise for future work is how much the assumption of fixed number of blocks $b$ can be relaxed. Our consistency results are robust to moderate increases in the number of blocks but cannot hold when $b=p$ for example. Another interesting direction of research is understanding the benefit of external information for parameter estimation and estimation error. \paul{Adapting a proof in Rigollet's high-dim statistics notes I could easily give here a bound on estimation error in the Gaussian sequence model. I don't how tight it is though. For linear regresison, there is proof for BIC in Rigollet's notes and I need to check more closely if it can be adapted. Do you know David a proof for the estimation error with $\ell_0$ selection in linear regression? Would it be interesting/relevant to have those bounds here?} \paul{We could also mention externally informed $\ell_1$ in correlated deigns as future direction.}
\david{Castillo, Schmidt-Hieber and van der Vaart have work on estimation error for the sequence model and linear regression, using posterior model probabilities that are analogs to our pseudo-posterior probabilities. I suppose that one could adapt their proof strategy. Miquel Torrens also studied MSE's in the sequence model, his proof strategy was different and more along the lines of what you did here. This said, I'd probably leave this for future work. It might be an easy-ish paper, perhaps.}

We also observe that our results bear similarities with conclusions of works on related problems. In Section \ref{sec:examples}, we discussed how the benefits of externally-informed selectors depend on how discriminative is the external information. \cite{scarlett2012compressed} similarly showed that support recovery in compressed sensing becomes easier as the entropy of the distribution of inclusion probabilities across blocks shrinks. In hypothesis testing, \cite{genovese2006} demonstrated that the power of their informed weighted testing procedure increases when assignment of weights is positively associated with the null hypotheses being false. We expect analogous results to be achievable in other related problems like structural learning in graphical models or variable selection over partially ordered sets as introduced in \cite{taeb}.
